, Sir Robert Gresley, of Drakelow Hall, viho was married on Wednesday to the late Duke cf Marl- borough's eldest daughter, was a pleasa4 looking bridegroom, tall and slight, and only eight and twenty. He has been a baronet for no fewer than &ix and twenty years. Drakelow is on old place on low ground close to Burton-on-Trent. Its black oaken staircase gives its interior a peculiar charm. The feature of the house is the collection of china which is displayed in the oaken glass-faced cup- boards that line the gallery.

Sir Charles Todd first heard the round of Bow Bells in 1826, and soon after attaining his majority he secured a post at Greenwich Observatory. Seven years later he was in charge of the Gal vanic.lle- partment of the Royal Observatory, which shows that he must have been a smart young man. So the Colonial Office must have thought, for they sent him out to South Australia as Government Astrono- mer and Superintendent of Telegraph. His great monumental feat out there has been the construc- tion of the overland telegraph line across the Australian Continent.

On the 20th instant is announced to appear in volume from " Le Docteur Pascal," by Emile Za This is the tu entieth and last of that remarkable series, " Les Reugon Macquart," the series which in- cludes such masterpieces as " L'Assommoir," " Nana,' and "Le Reve. Zola's scheme of depict- ing the fortunes of a family through all its branches was not a novel one. Balzac, in his "Cometie liumaine," utilises the same idea, but it Ltpretty certain that no previous. writer has ever presented' so many phases of life, in the form of fiction, as l. nla.

As far as the mere matter is concerned, most of his works would make three of the ordinary French isovels. In addition to the " Bougon-Macquart series, he has written numerous other volumes ot tales and essays,and yet he is but fifty-three years of age. bving been born in 1840. He was a singular failure in early life, and was plucked in his BA. t exam. He was at one time an employee of Hachette et Cie, the pttlishers, and it was here he submitted his first M.S7(a poem) to the head of the firm. The kind!): encouragement and advice he re- ceived from Georgbs Ilachette, bad, no doubt, a j bent ficial influence °dial& after career.

It is natural that the general should be well acquainted with the demeanor of thd Primate on important occasions, but it is interesting !-€) know that when he is in residence at Addington, his ecclesiastical life is pre-eminently simple and unostentatious. His family pew in Addington Parish Church is an ordinary hard-backed bench, innoc, at of mat, or chshion. Should his grace happen to reach the church a little late—an inci- dent which, it must be confessed, occasionally chances—he will even not trouble to disturb the general body of worshippers, but will sit down atnengst the villagers near the door,

There is an item in the lateA news from the Anti- podes that will delight Sir. Labouchere and the Radicals whp abominate ceremonious Parliamen- tary offices. In pursuance of the policy of rot:.each- went, the Victorian Goyernment has decidea to abolish the offices of Usher of the Black Rod and serjeant-a!-arms. This is the practical effect of the decision, but in order not to give the British Con- stitution too sudden and Severe a shock, the Prime Minister gently broke the news by intimating that these offices " will no longer exist for the discharge. of purely ceremonious functions,but will have other and more prosaic duties attached to them."

Australia gets two knights this deal—Sir John Madden and Sir Charles Todd, an Irishman and it Londoner. The former first saw the light in what the "Times" loves to call •• Rebel Cork," but in his boyhood he eccompanied his parents to the Anti- podes. The family settled near Melbourne, and in Sr. Patrick's College of that city the new knight eGrameneed his education, proceeding to the Mel- bowne University, where he carried all before him and became its first L L.D. Dr. Madden was returned to the Victorian Parliament by the largely birh constitueney of West Bourke, and he held office in two Governments as Minister of Justice. But he threw up politics ten years ago, and built up a big practice of the Melbourne Bar. Early this yesr he was chosen to succeed the late Chief Jus- tice Ifigginbwham.

A diversion was caused during question time ih the llonse'of Commons on Monday by the entrance into the Distinguished Strangers' Gallery of a trio of gorgeous Chinamen, attired in sky blue robes, with black caps decorated with crimson tassels and but- tons. They were all spectacled. Two of them w.-re grizzled mandarins, and between this elderly pair sat a younger Mongolian aristocrat. A ripple of amusement passed round the chamber as this young Chinaman eagerly protruded his hand be- yond the gallery and pointed out Mr. Gladstone to his companions. The latter were either short- sighted, or some obstacle interposed between them and the Prime Minister, for animated queries were addressed by thrm to their young friend, who ges- ticulated vigorously in the direction of the.Trea- : sury bench. At last Mr. Gladstone rose to speak, and then the pantomime in the Distinguis%ed Strangers' Gath,ry abruptly terminated. Celestial curiosity WAS gratified by both seeing and hearing the G.O.M. of world-wide renown.

Although his name is inseparable from Southern sport, yet Mr. Stoddart was horn in South Shiel.is, Durham, 29 years ago. end in his younger claws picked up a good deal of his cricketing knowledge at St. John's-wood School. About eight years ago his remarkable performances on behalf of the Hampstead Club attracted general attention, and he was quickly marked out as a coming man, for no less than fire centuries were placed to his credit for the suburban club in Tnly and August, 1885.

MOGRESSIVE BEAITIIES.

TEE FORTUNE-TELLER.

• In ancient times, oracles were so ambiguously constructed as to have reference to an event, how- ever it might terminate; and those who consulted. them ignominiously perished at the perfed they believed they wire to be triumphant. Heathen ceremonies have raned away, and neither flights of birds nor entrails of aii.mtils prognosticate the good or ill success of noalern enterprise, yet in the present day, when tilers is a light by which all rn-ty be directed, the infatuation of consulting fortnne•tellors and clairvoyants pteses.4:-.. to a (Its- graueiul extent. In the metropolia ;hey are nunikirous, a.itliough to thinking peesons". itxtune- Lenin_ is a. subject of abhorrence, rendered odious by belly,' opposed to common-sense, moial feeling, and the dictates f Christianity. Tha practice of consulting these is followed the most deplorable consequence. What mind eats be more degraded than that which can solicit from an unworthy beirsg any information concern- ing its future welfare ! The confessing of relating to such creatures any particulars of private import is not only repugnant to every ciceot 0114 idea, but it is a revolting indelicacy, and a positive surren- der of all that constituters rationality. Who are ttte individuals assuming the possession of super- natural wisdom? They are robbers in the worst acceptation of the term, whom the laws of the cotry condemn to the treadmill, and whom the intelligent portion of the community consign to infamy and detestation more opprobrious than another, it is that of fortune-teller.

WONDERFUL WATCHES.

In 1883 a watchmaker of Dresden exhibited a watch made entirely of paper. ,The paper was chemically prepared, and the maker claimed that it would be as serviceable as if made of the material ordinarily used. An ingenious prisoner at Kariaus, Bohemia, recently constructed a watch three inches in ' diameter, with no other tools or material except two needles, a spool of thread, a newspaper, and some rye straw. The wheels, posts and cogs, are all made of the rye straw, which, it is well known, is quite con se and tough. I. runs six hours with. out winding, and keeps good time. It is novii. in , the possession of Use Prefect of Kariaus, who cos it the greatest Mania of the nineteenth

Thewmost ingenious, perfect, and complex watch ever manufactured was sent out from the works of „„ratticj Philippe and Co., of Geneva, Switzerialid, in July, 1847, ATLI 11_112w in possess:on of TIM Nicholas. Ifis wEialT. is 1.-ZoTiii as a full-sized " On one side there is a dial of the 61.-eru lati (in kind, exhibiting the hour, minute, and second hands; also an independent chronograph ' hand, which marks the fifth of seconds. The same *Ojai has repcitino mechanism, which strikes the hems, qthiners and minutes. .pposite dial has hands pointing to months, neeki, and dirt. Another large central hand on this dial, if set at the beginning of each year, correctly points to all moon phases, and also a....ts as a perpetual calendar. It-is so constructed as to admit of its keeping two different times, say standard time on one side and sun time on the other. $ SLANG OF ARTISTS.

A party of artists were travelling in a stage, in trhich boside themselves, a sedate venerable lady was the only passenger. The conversation among the artists ran on as follows :—"How 'playful' those clouds are !—that group to the left is sweetly composed, though perhaps a little too ro/itt and rocky for the others. I have Seen nothing of s lately. I think he is clever. He makes all his flesh too chalky. You must allow, however, that • • he is very successful with his ladics.w The old lady began to exhibit symptoms ot un- easiness, and at the close of each oirservation cast an anxious and inquiring look at the speaker. Her companions, however, unconscious of the alarm they were exciting (for she entertained doubts as to their sanity), went on in the same style. She heard them, to her increasing dismay, talk of a farm house corning oat from the neighbouring trees, and of a gentleman's grounds wanting rep se. At length they approadiert an old village church. A greA.t. many observations were made about the 4 keeping.' o: the scene, which the old lady bore with toierable magnanimity; but at la_st one of the party exclaimed, in a kind • of enthusiasm: "See how well the woman in the red cloak carries toff the tower." This was too much. The lady • screamed to the coachman to stop, paid him his fare, although advanced only half way on „her Journey, and expressed her thankfulness for having escaped alive Irons such a set of madmen.

THE

LADIES' LETTER. . —.— MT DEAREST NELLrE, • There does not appear to be anything very In the make of mantles and jackets. Short, full capes are still in the ascendant, and one of the latest and prettiest make is of black satin, leach- ing just to the points of the shoulder. It has long wide satin ribbons in front, and these are passed round the figure, tying at the back. There is a full over-cape of e.;:u guipure, and the cap can be Heed with pale pink brocade. A novel shape in wipes is that arranged to reach the.waist.line only, or agiiin just above it, thus showing the figure to advantage; these look well when made of black diagonal cloth, with double shoulder capes of black wain. Another cape has a yoke with a high collar of satin, with a friti.ot the time bordering the base of the yoke. The skirt ia then made of cloth or serge; in each instance the capes are lined through with a coloured silk.

Sailor hats ere mach worn this season especially with eerge gowes, and the newest sciapes have rather low eei/iens ?LTA moderately wide brims. They are often ttiolueel with a hand of black satin ribbot end a Low of the same, with a couple of coloured v. ingeat the ilt, but a novel trimming, although one which does -not appear much worn• at present, is a band of self coloure I straw. These bands aro made in Sei;z rlenkl and are composed of a band of green (moss) atrAW plait encircling the Crown, whilst a hutl.siiy bow of the Wine material is placed in the front of the hat. This trimming is neat and should became popular. To make a stylish dress of a fancy woollen material with an indistinct check, the skirt should be gored, and jest slightly stiffened round the hem by the Insertion of a nine-inch strip of crinoline muslin between the material and the lining, and it should be bordered with a two-inch cross-way fold of brown velvet. Have the bodice short-waisted with a wide-folded band of velvet and a shoulder- frill of the same. The waistband should be _made. of a piece of velvet about eix-inches wide, unlined, the lower edge of which is sewn to the band until It is only about three-inches wide, and when they , are hooked together they form a kind of long rosette, somewhat resembling a cockscemb. The width in front and at the ..idos will be found to go Into natural folds withent my trouble wben the skirt is hooked over the bodice. A Chinese Idea of Beauty. ,- There really seems to be no end to the queer things which are done by the women of other countries to make their faces conforn to their own ideas of female beauty. The feet, as you know,are deformed in China and Japan, and in some countries the women go so far as to press their children's beads between boards, so as to make them hat, because they consider fiat heads pretty, and far superior in beauty to the natural shape. The Indian women thickly smear their heads with bear's fat. The Turkish women, almost without exception, colour their hair red. In Greenland the women- colour their faces blue and yellow. The women and children of Gazurat stain their teeth black, while the ladies of India and Japan gild their teeth and make them as brilliant as possible. But it remains for the Chinese to do some- thing which we in our country would consider very bar- barous and cruel indeed, and it is this : The Chinese do not admire small eyes, and to reach their standard of beauty a girl's eyes must not only be very large, but very long and brilliant. The eyes are easily made bril- liant by putting certain drugs in them ; but to make them long when nature bas not intended them to be so is quite another matter. To accomplish it the women have often recourse to a kind of home surgery which must be very painful. A Chinese mother, who has a child affected with small eyes, takes the child at the age of ten or twelve and slits the eyelid at the corner, just where it joins the lower lid. A very small slit is made, and this is then left to heal. As soon as it is healed it is slid again. Ani this process is continued until the eyes appear very long and prominent.

REMARKABLE DISCOVERY IN

PALESTINE.

REFORE THE ISRAELITES INVADED CANAAIS

Professor Flinders Petrie, in 1890. convinced himself that in a remarkable mound called Tel-el-Hey, in Southern Palestine, would be found the remains of what was one of the strongest places in the country down tc the invasion of Sentacherib and Nebuchadnezzar. The explorations s.oid Mr. Bliss at the Palestine Exploration Fund meeting, on Tuesday, have fully verified the fore- cast,the mound of To-1-el liesy being composed of no fewer that eight successive towns, formed on the ruins of one another, the uppermost dating about 520 a c., and the lowest, or earliest, going back to 1,500 n.c.—the time of the great Egyptian conquests of the land. The most important finds of all is a cuniform tablet; the first re- cord of.pre-Israelitish Canaan yut yielded up by the soil of Palestine, and Piofessor Sayce holds that it is but the forerunner of the library of the Governor of Lachish in days when the Irsaelitish invasion was still distant. Poofessor Sayce believes that this find heralds a discov- ery which will amount to" digging up thesources of the Book of Genesis." Amid all the evidence discovered by Mr. Bliss of the civilisation of that remote age—wine presses, treacle presses, alkali burninas, and innumer- able., others—by far the most curious is the disclosure of an iron blast furnace, so arranged as to give strong evi- dence of being intended to heat, in its descent, a blast of outside air forced through passages, before entering the chamber at the level where tuseres are usually found. If this theory be ecrerect (says Mr. Bliss), we find 1,400 years before Christ the use of the hot hair blast in- stead of cold air, which is called a modern improvement in iron manufacture due to Neilson, and patented in 1828.

OLD LO N D BRIDGE.

The first bridge thrown over, the Thames was a wooden one, erected by the nuns of St. Mary's Monastery, a consent of sisters endowed by the daughter of a rich Thames ferryman. The bridge figures as a forlified place in the early Danish invasions ; and the Norwegian Prince Olarnearly dragged it to pittees in trying to disposes the Danes, who held it in 1008. It was swept away in aft )od, and Rs euccessor was burnt. In the reign of •Henry IL, Pions Peter, a chaplain of St. Mary Colechurch, in the I Poultry, built a stone bridge a little farther west, and ! the king helped him with the proceeds of a tax on wool, I which gave rise to the old saying that " L;aedon Bridge I was built upon woolpaeks." Peter's bridge w:•s a curious structure, with nineteen pointed arches and a draw-. bridge. - There was a fortified gatehouse at each end„ and a Gothic chapel towards the cent re, dedicated to St. I Thomas Becket, the spurious martyr of Canterl.ury. In Queen E:iz Meth's reign there were slims nu ( itii r side, with flat roofs, arbours, and gardens, and at the south end rose a great fotir-storey wooden lioue, brought from Holland, which was covered with carving and gilding. In the Middle Ages, London Bridge was the scene of affrays of all kinds. Soon after it was built,the houses upon it caught fire at both ends, and 3,000 persons perished, wedged manning the flames. Henry 111. was driven back here by the rebellious Da Montfort, E.trl of Leicester. Wat Tyler entered the city by London Bridge and, later, Richard 11. was received here with gorgeous , ceremonies. It was the scene of one of /leery V.'s greatest triumphs, and also of his stately funeral proces- sien. Jack Cade seized London Bridge,and as lie psssed, elashed in two the ropes of the drawbridge, though soon 1 after his head was stuck on the gatehouse. From this bridge the mbel Wyatt was driven by the guns of thel Tower ;and in Elizabeth's reign waterwerks were erected !n the bridge. There was a great eonflsgration on the I bridge in 1032. andeaventually the Great Fire almost • destroyed it. In the Middle Ages countlt S 3 rebels' beads wpm stuck on the gatehouses of London Bridge. Brave Wallace's was placed there; and so were the heads of Henry VIII.'s victims—Fisher, Bishop of Rochester, and Sir Thomas Aline, thelatter trophy being carried off by the stratagem andelds brave daughter. Garnet, the Giippowder-PlotJesult, also contributed to the ghastly i triumphs of justice. Several celebrated painters, includ- ing Ilesarth, lived at one time or another on the bridge, and Swift and Pope used to frequent the shop of a witty bookseller, who lived under the northern gate. One or two celebrated suicides have taken place at London Bridge, and among these we may mention that of Sir William Templ.'s son, who was Secretary of War, and Eustace Budgell, a broken-down author, who left behind him as an apology the following sophism : What Cato dal and, Addison approved of cannot hi rong. Pleasanter is it to remember the anecdote of the brave apprentice, who leaped into the Thames from the win- dow of a house on the bridge to save his master's infant daughter; wimm a careless nurse hid stropped into the river: When the girl grew up, many noble suitors came,. but the generous father was obdurate. ‘ No," said the honest citizen ; " Osborne saved her, and Osborne shahl have her." And so he had ; and Osborne's great-grand- son throve, and became the first Duke of Leeds. The frequent loss of lives in shooting the arc!ses of the old bridge, where the fall was at time five feet, led at last to a cry for a new bridge, and one was commenced in 1824. Rennie designed it, and in 1831 William IV. and Queen Adelaide opened it. One hundred and • twenty thousand tons of stone went to its formation. The oil bridge was not entirely removed till 1832, when the' bones of the builder, Pious Peter of Colechurch, were found in the crypt of the central chapel, where tradition had declared they lay. The iron of the piles of the old bridge was bought by a cutler in the Strand, and pro- duced steel of the highest quality. Part of the old stone was purchased by Alderman Harmer, to build his house, Ingress Abbey, near Greenkithe.

CHAPTER XXXVII. AN ACCIDENT. Adjoining the Darkwood estate was a magnificent property, of several hundred acres, known as Beechmont. It was divided after the usual fashion into farrus, park, pastures, and meadows all in the highest state of cultivation. Its gardens were, noted for their beauty and extent, its conserva- tories, greenhouses, • and 'forcing houses were only inferior to those of Dunhohn Castle. Beechmont • was a freehold estate, and had belonged to an iron manufacturer, who had torn down the old mansion that had been "occupied by previous owners, and bu:(1. erected in its place a villa of -composite., architecture,, marked by a pro- fusion of weueoiesee-oriel, bay; Freuch, Greene, and Ca.therineewlieel—all so arranged as to har- monize, and toyreeent a picturesque and claming whole. The pavilions and colonnades of Beechmont •••-• features of the place. Its long walks 'arching trees, its secluded galleries, its gre. , entrance porch, were all of marked iuteresi. .. beauty: The wealthy-iron manufacturer, who had lavished a fortune upon this place, which had seemed perfect when he had bought it, had died some months before the accession of Lord Darkwood to his title and estates. The iron manufacturer had left a large family of sons and daughters. Under his will It was necessary to divide the property into several portions, and, as frequently happens in like cases; not one of the soils could afford to keep Beechinont in his share. It was offered for sale, and bad been be the market several months without having found a purchaser. Now, Lord Darkwood had conceived the idea of purchasing Beechniont, and adding it to his already overgrown estate. His predecessor had not ex- pended one fourth his income during his adminis- tration of affairs, and ielailei sum of ready money lay in the Bank of England, subject to his lord- ship's call; but, considering the price of the pro- perty too high, he had affected indifference, had pretended to think better of the project, and had artfully advised those in charge to sell to some one else if opportunity offered. These tactics met with an unexpected result. Mr. Sutton wrote to Lord Darkwood, informing him that another purchaser was in the field. The Marquis replied promptly that that device was too old and threadbare to deceive him, and that, if any one else desired to buy Beechmont, at the price fixed upon it, that person waswelcorne to it. To his lordship's great amazement and -chagrin, Mr. Sutton's reply to this letter was that Beech- mont was sold, and that the new purchaser would enter into immediate possession. • This information arrived at Duraholm Castle a day or two after the events narrated in the pre- ceding chapter. The question that now agitated the soul of Lord Darkwood was this: Who had bouget Beechmontet Some speculator who desired to sell again at an advance, a retired tradesman, or some offshoot of nobility with whom be might become intimate? He wrote to Mr. Seams, and was answered that the new owner of Beeclunont was a lady, that she was unmarried, and newly returned from India. , "A yellow begum," said Lord Darkwood, dis- contentedly; "a stout, elderly woman, with a net parrot, Himloo servants, a beak nose, and dis- agreeable ways. I wish I had fallen iu with their terms." But in the course of a week the agent of the new proprietor of Beechmout appeared at time mansion and engaged a staff of servants, and made ready for the home-coming of his employer. And then Pietro, who was wont to hear every- thing, made the acquaintance of the agent in ques- tion, and came to his master with the information that the owner of Beechmont was Mies Norreys, a young Indian heiress, and that her father had recently died In ludia, leaving her a very great fortune, and that she had returned to Eugland to spend the remainder of her life. "And to marry," added Lord Darkwood. "That follows, of course. I wonder if she is engaged ? If not, she won't remain unengaged a long tune, not if she were hideous as Medusa. With Beechmont at her back, and money besides in the funds, she can marry almost whom she will." " Why don't you enter the race, signor ?" suggested Pietro. "The holy is young; rich, and well cenneeted._ She will take her place in county society at once. She would not be a bad match even for you." . CHAPTER XXXVIII.

terranean passage for more than an hour. Inen he came once more to the door, and, after looking round to see that he Was not observed, but for- getting in his hurry to lock the door again alter him, made his way cautiously to the spot where he had left his horse.

----iiii-;;;;;o-o-n-1;;;:ck at the castle again; and, hav- ing given out that he had simply been for a scamper along the roads, went up -into- the library. lie found, as he Mtpected, Pietro awaiting him there, and a long conference was held between the two. Whether Lord Dark wood had attained his object 01' not, did not appear. • Matters took -their usual course, anti at about eleven o'clock his lordship pleat* fatigue and returned to his room. In less ' than dn hour all the lights in the castle were ex- tinguilihed, and apparently every one was at rest withi4 its walls. Buti just before midnight, when the moon was hidden by a passing (loud, two figures muffled in cloaks issued from r • • postern and made their . way tinier the shadi. 'le trees and lofty wahla. rouudito the ruins. As may be surmire were none other than Pieta. d his master, Ta . ~quipped themselves carat y for their noe. .edition. ' They had just,r clied the massi‘ " doorway, and were about to plunge into th -s of its shadow for the piirpose of opening Li. , when Pietro sud- denly kilutchecl his master's ~.. in, and drew him on on`eide into the deep gloom of the trees. '‘ IT st, signor," he whispered—"voices." Lord Darkwood followed the supple Italian with- a muttered curse. - ' -•- ." Spne scoundrelly poachers," he whispered in 4-1 reply, auur then they both remained silent. It Was well they did so, for hardly had the words.l left I.rd Darkwood"s lipi when the light of the moon Shone out brightly, converting the small open space in front of the doorway into a silvery moil- . . ance, and making everything as clear and distinct, , as in broad daylight. 2.- ~, What was the surprise and consternation of the', '- confederates to see the door slowly open, and two. figures, one unmistakably that of a Woman—the sea of the other, who was wrapped in a long, eastern' .I'' looking robe, was doubtful—emerge into the moon-, , light. They passed quite close to where the,,l marquis and the valet lay concealed; but their,,, heads were turned away, and their features could', i not be -distinguished. • ~, There was no difficulty in discerning, however, . that one was a young and evidently very graceful ~,, woman, not only from the sweep of the robe but, 4 from the erect carriage and quick movements. Her 1 companion carried a lantern, and was evidently en- deavouring to dissuade her from some course she ..,1 was earnestly bent upon pursuing. '1 As they drew near, Lord Darkwood caught one . of the voices—that of the bearer of the lantern. It'' was rich and manly in tone, but quite unknown to-- him, • "When, dearest, in this secret that overhange us both. to be cleared up for ever?" it said.. ..., -" When will this miserable spying and wandering cease, and why should yon of all the world help me thus, in what I feel must be a task as hopeless as to gain the love of one of yonder bright stars?" "Nay, Ronald," was the gentle reply, "be a little more patient, and all will yet be well. The cloud will pass away as'quickly as that which but n_ew spreads its gloom over the face of the moon. How fortunate that I found the door of yonder dreary place open • even now--" The remainder of the sentence was lost in the distance, and Lord Darkwood, peering forth from his hiding place, saw the youth, for he seemed little more, twine his arms lovingly round his fair coin- Panieei's waist as they turned into the put!' which led down to the high road to Shrewsbury,. As soon as they were out of sight Lord Darkwood and his companion turned towards the ruins. Neither spoke, for the name of Roland, so lovingly uttered by the girl, had given them both food Tor deep reflection, and neither wished to betray to the' other the emotions which the mention of that name- - had roused. Was the girl who had smitten in such soft and ' tender accents Lord Darkwood's eldest daughter, the child of poor, wronged Clare Markham, and was her companion the Sir Roland Chilton of whom both had heard so, much? If so, what did their presence at that hour at Dunholm, and by the rn4lis of the old castle, mean? Pietro was the first to recover his composure, "A couple of village lovers, signor, lesupposc whom we have startled. You must send a keeper - or two over here; but how came you, after all eV caution, to leave open that door t" This solution of the problem had not occurred to his lordship; but he jumped at it as drowning men Catch at straws. "Not a doubt of it, Pietro. It shall- be looked to; but it gave me an awful fright at first. . How I came -to be guilty of the insensate folly of not. locking the gate I cannot imagine. I was under the impression that I did so, and, even now belief& that I did. There may be duplicate -keys, whiab makes matters still worse. But we have no time to lose if we are to complete our task. Come." Both would gladly have followed the mysterious pair who had disturbed them; but they were afraid to trust each other, and were forced to let whAt - both in their heart of hearts believed to be a golden Opportunity_ for finding Gwen slip through their' fingers.' Whatever their business was that tot them to the ruins, it did not occupy them long, an It was plain that they accomplished it to their mutual satisfaction, for when they parted inside the postern gate, each to make his way to his own apartment, the marquis turned round to Pietro, and laying his hand on his valet's shoulder said, in a low voice— . . "I have ranch, Pietro, to thank you for in ,n3Y life, 'and in nothing more than in this night's work. You will be no loser by it." The valet bowed gratefully; but there was 6 malicious gleam of triumph in his eyes, and fla muttered to himself— " Take care my lord marquis, that when our (10 _ of reckoning comes you are not the loser." - (To be continued). ,

Alderman HOLMES had listened to many objections which had been raised, some of them being of an ex- traordinary character. The debate was also of the most extraordinary character he had ever heard. STREET IMPROVEMENT COMMITTEE:.

--- Alderman Strrrox proposed the adoption of the Street improvement Committee's minutes. Councillor MUIR seconded the motion. • Councillor FLEMING asked if that Committee hail the , right to permit the hanging of " sky-signs " in the streets. Their existence was a standing danger to life. He had some time Ago mentioned the dangers of grids on the footpath, and he hoped in drawing their attention to the dangers ot these signs more heed would be paid Ito the matter. Alderman SUTTON explained that the Committee could I not prevent the signs being hung. The staff at the hospital wculd bc gradually reduced and the expenses brougkt down as low as possible. Alderman CHANDLEY, in submitting the minutes sf the Town Hall, Park and Baths Committee, said the report dealing with the centralization ef municipal offices had been submitted to the General Purposes Committee. So far the baths appeared to have given ur.iversal satisfaction. The committee wished to express their thanks to the Borough Surveyor for his services in connection with the baths ; his trouble and difficulties were simply unknown. (Hear, hear.) No one in Warrington knew better than he (the Alder- man) did, the immense amount of trouble Mr. Longdin had gone through. He had worked very hard to bring about the present state of things, and on one occasion he was up until three o'clock in the morning pushier on operations. His services were -very much appreciated by the committee. (Hear, hear.) Councillor BOLTON seconded the motion. The Fire Engine Committee have accepted the tender of Messrs. Merryweather and Son to supply a No. 2 Greenwich pattern steam fire engine for £525 ; the two manual engines to be advertised for sale. WATER COMMITTEE.

Alderman Fasscoms, in moving the confirmation of the minutes of th!s committee, referred to the resolution dealing with the Ship Canal Committee, which read as follows :—" The Manchester Ship Canal Company hay- refused to pay the Corporation a lump sum for the lay- ing of the pipes through the subways at Twenty Step Bridge, resolved that Mr. Ross be Instructed to put the work in hand and charge the company with the cost." The Ship Canal Company did not exactly agree with their terms, but the committee had since instructed Mr. Ross to go on with the work of laying the pipes under Twenty Step Bridge. He thought the company would agree to pay the cosE, whatever it was. (Hear, hear.) A deputation from the Infirmary having waited on the committee upon the subject of obtaining a better supply of water for fire extinguishing purposes at the Infirmary, it was resolved that the Win wick main be extended from Tanners-lane through Bewsey-street and Edge- worth-street to Legh-street. The committee never met now, without considering means to provide better appli- ances for the extinction of fires in the borough, and the present was part of a scheme which would ultlinately run throughout the town. Since then the committee had spent much money to improve that state of things, as they were anxious to make the town as secure as possi- ble from fires. With regard to Chat Moss, Professor Boyd Dawkins said he was of opinion that the proposed works of the Manchester Corporation would not affect the water supply of Warrington. The committee were afraid some of the filth might drain into their water env/4y, but the Professor said it would not be injured at all. lie said,however,that the contaminated water would find its way into the adjacent streams, the Glazebrook and the Dwell, along the surface, along with the other surface waters. They would, therefore, get sadly too much of it under Warrington Bridge. The Alderman then proceeded to refer to the excellent report of the Town Clerk on the financial aspect of the water works, published in last Saturday's Examiner. _ . .

Alderman Surron alluded to the supply of water to Penketh, and remarked that he could not see where the profits would come in in some oases for the committee, if they agreed to supply the village on the terms suggested. It seemed to him that the Rural Sanitary Authority would be the gainers and the committee the losers by the present arrangement. Perhaps he was wrong. Councillor ROBERTS suggested that the resolution of the Water Committee should be altered to read as follows : " The committee having received an application from the Warrington Rural Sanitary Authority for the supply of water to Penketh, resolved that the necessary mains be laid and the supply furnished, upon the Rural Sani- tary Authority agreeing to guarantee to the Corporation an annual payment at the rate of 13i per cent, on the capital outlay, such sum to he reduced from time to time by an equitable proportion of the amount received for the water supply to the district." That would leave the question entirely open. The TOWN CLERK said that by law the inhabitants of any part of the district might demand a supply of water at a charge not exceeding ten per cent, on the cost of the .outlay, and the committee came to the conclusion, after discussing various points, that Si percent. would be sufficient to cover the amount..

Councillor Romners : I am satisfied after the explana- tion of the Town Clerk.

The minutes were then agreed to..

The adoption of the mintites of the General Purposes Committee, without discussion, concluded the business of the meeting.

FINANCE.

On the recommendation of the Finance Committee a number of accounts amounting to over £2OO were passed chiefly the salaries of teachers. The balance at the bank to meet these accounts was stated to be only £35, and it was decided to ask the overseers to make a pay- ment on account of the precept. _ _

. _ THE EVENING SCHOOLS. The Clerk presented the report of H. M. Inspector ' with respect to the evening schools. The Inspector 1 states : " This school is taught with zeal and ability and the results of the examination in both elementary and class subjects are highly satistactorv." The number of times the school was open was 63 and the average attendance 39.5. There were 46 pupils presented for examination ; and there were 48 passes in reading, 45 in writing, 37 in arithemetic, 30 in Engli!h, 9, algebra; 10, mensuration; 25, history. The total grant was £32 4s.— The Clerk also presented a statement of the income and expenditure for the year ended April 30th 1893. The income was £52 83. 4d., and the expenditure reached exactly a similar sum.—Mr. Handley pointed out that £1 9s. 4d. only was required from the rates, so that virtually the evening schools paid for themselves.— The Clerk pointed out that a new code for evening schools had been issued, which would come into operation for next session. He had noted some distinctive features which would be of interest to intending pupils. In the first place there was an extended curriculum and an entire freedom of choice of subjects. Secondly there was a new class subject, viz. : " The life and duties of the citizen." Thirdly. no ! scholars would be compelled to take the elementary subjects—reading, writing and arithmetic. Fourthly, the annual examination was abolished. Fifthly, the Inspector would visit the school with or without notice once or twice during the session and report as to the general efficiency of the school. Sixthly, the grant would be based on the numb.ar of hours devoted to each subject and variable from Is. or is. 811., according to the Inspector's report.—Mr. Imison : There is no individual examination then.—The Clerk: No.—Mr. Imison observed that it was practically the abolition of pay- ment by results. This was the whole of the buisness:

MUNICIPAL AMENITIES AT WIGAN.

At the monthly meeting of the Wigan Town Council, held on Wednesday, Alderman Ackerley moved a resolu- tion, that in the opinien of the Council, the conduct of Councillor Woods in attacking him at a public meeting was derogatory to the dignity of the Council. At a meeting in the Drill Hall, Councillor Woods said in a speech that Alderman Ackerley was the " great mercen- ary of the Tory party, and as clerk of the union received more money from the rates thon any pauper, and it was perhaps fortunate for him that he did -so."—Alderman Ackerley. referring to the remarks made by Councillor Woods, said that he had not received Is-for his services to his party since he had taken a public position. He considered that it was unfair that a member of the Coun- cil should be gibetted before a public meeting, and his personal character bespattered with mud.—Councillor Woods was of opinion that personal matters should net be brought into the Council. They would soon be hav- ing inquiries as to whether certain aldermen and coun- cillors, were sober after Corporation banquets, and they would be the laughing stock ef the whole country if these personal matters were allowed.—Councillor Farri- mond : What are we now ?—Councillor Richard Johnson spoke in defence of Councillor Woods. Com- menting on the ruling of the Mayor on one occasion, he said he was a partisan Mayor.—Councillor Kellet : It's a lie. (" Order,order."j—Councillor Johnson ultimately withdrew the remark,adding that he thought the Mayor endeavoured to be a non-partisan.—Alderman Acker- ley was ultimateiy allowed to address the Council again, and explained that Councillor Woods had displayed a reckless malignity in his attack when he had referred to his connection with the Primrose League, which, he contended was a body independent of the Conservative party of the town. He admitted that as provincial secretary of the league he received a salary.—After several proposals for avoiding further discussion, the motion was put to the meeting, and carried by the Con- servatives, the Liberals refusing to vote.

TUESDAY.

In the House of Lords on Tuesday,. the Bishop of CUESTRR moved the second reading of the Authorised Companies (Liquor) Bill, which he said did not interfere in any way with personal freedom, but proposed to in- troduce into this country a system which had been suc- cessful in Sweden and Norway, and had been adopted in principle by the Federal Government of Switzerland. The licensing authority was untouched by the bill. The- Duke of ARGYLL said that if the motion for a second reading were passed to a • division lie should not vote against it. The Duke of WksvlnusTun believed that there was a general demand for further restrictions upon the sale of intoxicating liquors. The Marquis of SALIS- BURY said his opinion was that it would be better not to , press the secord.reading, but if it were pressed he should - not vote against it. The motion for the second reading was negatived without a division.

In thelliouse of COmmons, Sir Wm. HARCOURT, reply- ing to Mr. Hopwood as to the Board of Customs requir- ing applicants for the pot of boatmen to have been successfully vaccinated within the last seven years, said he should for the safety of his own family require those who lived In his house to be vaccinated, and, if neces- sary, re-vaccinated, and he was not prepared tcerecoro- mend a different practice in the public service..

Mr. Asum, replying. to Mr.S.eir Hardie regarding an allegation in. the "Pall Mall Gazette"tha.t the Postmaster General had won his seat by bribery,. said he did nat think anything had occurred which called upon the Public Prosecutor to take action—a remark which was greeted with loud laughter. Dr. Cr.knis introduced a bill which WAS read a first time to abolish the elective disabilities of women.. The House went into Committee on the Home Rule Bill, again taking uP sub-section ffaf Clause 3, which proposes to except from the legislative powers of the Irish Assembly "treason, treason-felon)), alienage, or naturalisation." Mr. BUTCHER proposed to insert after " treason-felony "the words" conspiracy and combina- tion." Mr. MORLEY defended the clause as it stood.and Mr. BALPOUR spoke in favour of the amendment. which was eventually negatived by a. majority of 41.

Mr. J. ?gloater said the Government would be willing to. insert the word " aliens," so that the sub-section before the committee wculd read " aliens, alienage„ or naturalisation." Mr. BRODRIcE. assented, and withdrew his amendment. Mr. T. Healey, Mr. Clancy, Mr. Seaton,. and Mr. Redmond spoke more or less strongly against the amendment pro- posed by Mr.. Morley. Mr.. GELDSSONS said the Govern- ment wanted to he perfectly certain that the words of the section were sufficient to keep in the hands of the Imperial Government and Imperial Parliament what- ever related to the fulfilment of Imperial obligations. He hoped the Nationalist members would accept the amendment. On a division, however, the Nationalists voted in a body against the Government. the Opposition voting with the Ministry, whose majority was 189, the amendment being csrrid. A verbal amendment, moved by Mr. Scx•rox, to insert after the word "alien" the words "as such," was rejected by a majority for the Government of 48. The Voluntary Conveyances Bill pissed through com- mittee, after a clause had been added, on the motion of Mr. S. MORLEY, extending its operation to Ireland. The Friendly Societies Act (1875) Amendment Bill was read a second time.

THERSDAY,

In the House of Lords on Thursday, the Weights and Measures Bill was read a second time. lArd PIATFAIR moved the second reading of the North Sea Fisheries Bill, which re-enacts, with a few verbal alterations, the Act sf 1880, for giving effect to the In- ternational Convention signed by Great Britian, Gera mans-, France, Denmark, and the Netherlands. Lord SALI;BOHY spoke in favour of the bill, which was read a second time. Lord BALFOUR of Burleigh moved an address pray-.inthat her Majesty's consent might be withheld from the minute of the Committee of Council in Education in 'Scotland relating to secondary education, dated lot of May, 1893. The motion was adopted by a rnakority of 59.

In the House of Commons, Sir WILLIAM [LWICOURT replying to Sir R. Temple and Sir R. Paget,, said the arrangements as to the superannuation scaeme for elementary teachers must depend on the resoucces at the disposal of the Government, the state of the revenue, and the money they found themselves able b. devote to the purpose. Mr. GLADSTONE replied to a series of ,Ipsstions sug- gestive of more rapid methods for dealing with amend- ments to the Home Rule Bill, remarking that the pressure of the Irish question did not absolve the Government from their obtigatioas to the cluntry and responsibility ' in respect of other great measures /tie Great Britain. The House went into Committee on the Rome Rule Bill, and on Clause 3 Mr. Bartley proposed to leave out all after" trade," and insert " bounties. to promote Irish industries." Mr. Gladstone opposed the amendment. and Mr. Balfour and Mr. Chamberlain took part in the discussion. The amendment use negatived. At a later stage, Mr. BALFOUR and Mr. Assenosa questioned the Chairman's ruling, and the persistency of Mr. Ambrose provoked an exciting scene. Mr. Gladstone suggesting that he should be named, Amid a scene of prolonged excitement the Chairman at length called on Commander Bethell, and Ms. Ambrose left the House with what is described as an insulting bow to the Chairman,

North Western Co., and £200,000 by the Great Western. Judging from a statement made in the Manchester City Council on Wednesday by Sir J. Ilaawoon there seems to be a disposition on the Part of the railway companies to delay the pro- gress of the canal works. Yesterday -at Runcorn Police Court, before lr. J. A FOROIVING WIFE AT WIDNES!.

Peter Kelly, of Widnes, was brought up under a war- rant on a charge of assaulting his wile.—Mr Knowles said he appeared for the defendant. He had seen com- plainant, who now desired to withdraw the charge. It appeared the man periodically gave way to drink. They had seven children. His client periodi- cally became a mad fool, and he had asked Mr. Taylor, the police court missionary, to allow him to sign the pledge. Further he had promised that he would ratify his promise with the parish priest.—Mrs. Kelly said she wished the charge to be withdrawn.—The Bench granted the request, and the Chairman expressed a hope that it would be a beginning of better things— that defendant would turn over a new leaf. NEW LIBERAL CLUB FOR RUNCORN.

Union Hall will on Wednesday next be re-opened as the home of ftuncorri Liberal Club. Extensive altera- tions are being carried out by Mr. W. Bankes, of High- t. The ground floor, consisting of a room of ample proportions, is to be devoted to billiards, an' which for the present will be occupied by two full-sivd billiard tables and one semi-billiard table. On the first floor is the newsroom, a commodious room, which will be sup- plied with newspapers and periodicals. There are also two other rooms, one of which will be utilised as secre- tary's office and the other as a committee room. Facing Regent-at. will be a balcony, from which speeches may be delivered to those assembled in the streets. Con- sidered in all aspects, the premises are superior to any previously occupied by the club, whose home will no doubt be fixed here for many years to come.

RUNCORN SONS OF ENPERANCE. At the fortnightly meeting ot this Society on Satin, day, under the presidency of Mr. Thomas J. Bolt, an interesting paper was given by Mr. David Smith on the Bible. In opening he said the Bible was the word of God, and as such was the rule of faith for Christians. He spoke of it as containing a message to men and then referred to Voltaire's threat that Christianity would 'shortly, be stamped out Yet it still lived ani Voltaire's house was now used as a depot for the Bible Society. Christianity had made marvellous progress. He pro- ceeded to say that the Bible was better than Shake- speare—it declared the brotherhood of men, and was a , lover to elevate and bless mankind, and calculated to maks men happy, and point them to a higher and nobler life. A discussion followed in which Messrs. Daniel, Bisbrown, Edward Key, Thomas Cooke and George S Sutton engaged. A vote of thanks to Mr. Smith was accorded.

THIC 110 ME IRON THADE. Business in several branches of the English Iron market exhibits a slight amelioration. THINGS .HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL COIIIINSPONDANT.

The magistrates' decisions in regard to private street making will meet with the general approval of the ratepayers. It- is only just that, before the town is saddled with the responsibility of keeping private streets in repair, they should be put in the hest possible condition. Those who, like Mr. Foulkes, have been left in undisturbed possession of their rents for 15 years without being called up- on to go to the expense erf making their streets ought to pass a vote of thanks to the Commission- ers for their kindness and leniency,and not grumble as though they were -being robbed. It is for those tenants who for 15 years have not had the accom- modation to which they are justly entitled, to grumble at the long delay.

--- Lively things may be expected at the meeting of Widnes Town Council on Tuesday next. On that day Alderman Sadler will bring forward his motion condemning Councillor Bullough's extraordinary speech at a football dinner, and calling upon the Council to affirm its confidence in the Corporation Committees.

I heard a good joke the other day with regard to Mr. George IV hiteley, M.P. for Stockport, once the idol of the Northwich Division Tories. It appears that a Runcorn gentleman, who is an enthusiastic follower of Lord Salisbury. opened a subscription list shortly after the General Election with the intention of presenting Mr. Whiteley with an address for the gallant fight he had made against Mr. Brunner. Some time ago Mr. Whiteley was communicated with to ascertain when it would be convenient for him to receive the tri- bute of his Runcorn admirers ; but the now full fledged M.P. having apparently no further need for votes in Northwich Division, or recognising that as his gallant fight ended in increasing Mr. Brunner's majority by two hundred, he was not entitled to any adoration or compliment, did not deign to reply to the communication. The prime mover in the affsir has this week been engaged returning sun- dry half guineas, dollars and half-crowns to their respective owners, and his disgust at Mr. Whiteley's treatment has been expressed in unmeasured terms.

Tire newly- united couple were the recipients of costly and numer- ous presents. These included a massive marble time- piece from the employ 6,4 at the tannery of Messrs. Ockleston, Halton-road, Runcorn. On Wednesday, in honour of the event, a quantity of bunting was dis- played in the neighbourhood at the works. Those en- gaged by Mr. Ockleston unanimously determined that they would postpone the rejoicings connected with the happy event until the return of their employer and his wife, when they could be accompanied by them on a trip to Blackpool, which it has been arranged shall take place at the beginning of July. It may be of interest to our readers to know that Mr. Miller occupies several important positions in the district in which he resides, and that he is exceedingly well known in busine-s circles in Scotland. The bridegroom belongs to one of the Molest families of Runcorn and district. He is the son of the late Mr. Ockleston, who for a long series of years conducted the business in which he has been succeeded by his sons. He is very popular, not only among his workpeople, hut with all with whom his manifold duties bring him in contract. The community generally join in the wish that the future of the happy pair may be attended with all the succeAs that they can desire.

DARING JEWELLERY ROBBERY AT ST.

HELENS.

A robbery of a daring nature wait perpetrated early on Wednesday tnorning at the shop of Mr. Joseph Hewitt, Jeweller and silversmith, Church- at., Sr. Helens. For many years Mr. lleivitt and his family lived on the. premises, but on aCConnt of his health he some time ago removed to the subur- ban district of St. Ann's. The block of property of which the shop forms part is situated at the corner of Ilardshaw-at., and during the past few weeks considerable altnationa have been going on. The shop was securely fastened on Tuesday night, but when Mr. Broadhead, the manager, went to open it on Wed- nesday morning ha found that a good many things had been disturbed, and a careful examination showed that an entrance had been effected through a cellar grate at the rear of the premises. The thieves, haring obtained access, ascended the steps, where they were confronted with a strong door,on the shop side of which were seve- ral strong bolts. Great power must have been used, for the door was forced open, and they were facs to face with a stock valued at several thousand pounds. A large portion of this had fortunately been placed in thief-proof safes, and could not be tampered with. Although there was a light burning at the far end of the shop from the street, and the view from the window at some points was a good one, they made a dash down of mixed gold and silver watches. 'Crossing to the other behind the counters,etriPPine' nearly the whole of a tray side of the shop they let down the hack of a case which was covered with a cloth, and which contained a large and varied quantity of jewellery. A handful of gold and diamond pearl rings were taken promiscuously from a tray, while a more c-eatly parcel of lady's brooches, which was close to, was missed. A tray of silver chains had been moved, but none had been taken, and a valu- able gold watch, made at the Present Watch Pitctory.fer which Mr. Hewitt is the Sr. Helena agent, had been placed handy, but it was left. It is supposed that the thieves were a [raid to stay, and made their exit through the cellar, carefully putting the grid down again. Until stock can be taken, the value of the inis.sing goods can- not correctly be estimated. hut, roughly speaking, it may amount to £l,OO or £.500, or even more. A few months since, it will he remembered. the pawntirokirg shoo of Mr. Lvi Pennington, fifty yards away, won entered in a similar mariner, and in 'that case a hasty retreat seemed also to have been made. Time robbery at Mr. Hewitt' seems to have been carefully planned, And carried out in it manner which cull only 1107 i been accomplished by either profeoienal "lifters" or wrsons graduating iii this direction.

THE PROFESSIONAL AUDITORSIIIP QUESTION AT WARRINGTON,

COUNCILLOR SLUM GREEN INTERVIEWED. After the proposal of the Finance Committee that Councillor Shaw Green should be appointed pro- fessional auditor for the borough was rej-cted by the Town Council on Tuesday evening, one of our reporters called upon Mr. Green and interviewed him upon certain points which were raised in the discoesion.

" It has been said," our reporter commenced " that the idea of having a prof essionalnuditor for the borough was first introduced by yourself, Mr. Green." "Oti,no Alderman Holmes was the author of the idea. My view was, on considering a re-arrangement of the way the Finance Committee's duties are performed to form from the Finance Coirunittee an audit sub- commiltee ; but of course I fell in with the Chairman's wish, because a professional auditor takes the entire responsibility upon iiirsiwilf." " In what way wink' the appointment of a profes- sional auditor affect the expense of auditing the accounts."

" It would make very little difference indeed for the salary paid to the professional auditor, and the aes which would then be payable to the elective audi- tors, together, we uld not be much more that the sum now paid to the elective auditors alone. 'fhe elective 'auditors would then only perform the duties defined by statute. At present they do more." . _ .

" I believe that many Corporations are now appoint- ing professional auditors." "Yes, that is so. The last incorporated borough— Widnes—has already appointecl Oflo who happen, to he a member of the Society of Incorporated Acc,untants, practising in Manchester." "[here has been some opposition raised to your appli- cation because you are a member of the Society of In- corporated Accountants r . .

"So I have beard." " What is the difference between that Society and the Institute of Chartered A.crount rots ?" " None. The Institute of Chartered Accountants is the dder body of the two, and ac)s under a charter granted by the Privy Council. The Society of Incorporated Accountants acts under license from the Board of Trade. Naturally, the Institute, being the elder body, has the , greater number of members. But during the last seven years In the House of Commons, before judges and registrars, the statue of the Society has been recognised as equal to that of the Institute. Each body is governed by • council, is an ex- ainining body, and exercises disciplinary powers. Members of both are in partnership in different places; and a member of each is in the Horse of Commons. During the present Session two Bills have been brought before the House for the organisa- tion of the profession, the second Bill, a copy of which I have before me, is the one which was promoted by the Institute of Chartered Accountants. recites amongst other things that the Bill makes provision for placing all the members of the society of accountants in their corresponding positions as members of the Institute. This is the strongest evi- dence of the statement I make above that there is no difference between them, for the Bill will amalgamate both Institute and society." "It is said it is necessary to undergo successfully an examination before a membership of the Institute can be obtained P" _ . .

"This applies equally to bath, and I have the impres- sion that the examinations of the Society are the harder of the two."

"Can you tell me whether there are any chartered accountants in Warrington who have not passed such an examaidation ?"

"I only know two who are in practice who have,and they are young men ; but I think two others have recently completed their chirkships. I think lam right in saying there are six members of the Institute in War- rington who have passed no examination." " Finally, is there any truth in the allegation that you created the office with the intention of filling it ?" "It is grossly untrue. I thought I was too well- known for any one to make such a suggestion."

WARRINGTON AND THE ROYAL WEDDING.

A public meeting of the ladies of Warrington, presided over by the Mayoress (Mrs. E. Greenall), was held in the Town Hall, on Thursday afternoon for the purpose of deciding upon some auitable way of commemorating the forthcoming royal wedding. Amongst the ladies who were present were :—Mrs. F. Monks, Mrs. Lyon Whittle, Mrs. Broadbent, Miss Broadbent, Mrs. Venn, Mrs. John Ilazton, Mrs. Harrison, Mrs. James Fairclough, Mrs. Long, Miss Long, Mies White ana others. It was decided that no present should be sent to the royal couple, but that the loyalty of the town should be expressed by a demonstration. It was suggested that there should be a procession t lirough the town of the local corps of the volunteers, and the regulars, also the Fire Brigade, and the local cyclists in costume. In connection with the latter, there should be prizes given for the best " make-up." For the purpose of raising funds to accomplish this, a subscription list was opened, and arrangements will be made for receiving donations at the local banks. The ladies who were present formed themselves into a com- mittee. A public meeting will be held on Monday night for deciding upon definite arrangements.

I DARING ROBBERY AT WARRINGTON YESTERDAY. A robbery of a most impudent character was par- petrated yesterday afternoon at the shop of Mrs. Cheaters, tobacconist, Bridge-st. About twenty minutes to two Miss Emily Chester, Mrs. Chester'e younger daughter, was in charge of the shop when a well dressed gentlemanly looking man of about 30 came in and asked if etie would be kind enough to give him a sovereigt. bra half sovereign and ten shillings in silver. Ile said he wanted to send lift money away' by post. Miss Chesters obliged him with the required change, but in doing so she took the money till from the drawer and placed it on the floor behind the counter. No sooner had she given the man the change than another we:l dresse I young man, with a very pleasant, suave manner came in. Ile had been standing at the door. Ile said : " Oh, Miss, will you kindly tell tlie the price of this terracotta in the window." Che-ters, never suspecting anything, walked out to the shop window and told the new comer what the prices were of the articles he named. Ile thanked her and promised t 3 call later on. Then he raised his hat and walked quickly away. Just as Miss Cheaters turned to go into the shop the man who asked for the change walked out. She took no pat ticular notice of him but walked behind the counter when she found that the till bad dis- appeared. It contained over .£3O of which .£25 was in money and the remainder in notes. So surprised and frightened was the girl that some moments elapsed before she could call her mother. When Mrs. Chester's saw the situation she rushed into the street to look for a policeman, but there were none in the vicinity. Several persons who were about were informed of the matter and a general impression got abroad that the theices had made away in the direction of Bank Quay. tic. several men rushed to Bank Quay and Arpley Stations, hut no traces of the ingenious robbers could be found. It was thought then that. they had gone on the Liverpool-road. and Mr. A. Booth, of Buttermarket-street, mounted a bicycle and rode off in the direction of Bold, hoping to overtake the men. Ile warned the Sankey and Penketh poliee as he went along, but on arriving at Bold he could ' }lnd no reasen for supposing the the thieves had passed that way,mn he made his wnyback to Warring ton. By the time he arrived there,however,a girl I.ad informed Mrs. Cheaters that she saw the two men rushing along Mersey-street with the till. Eventn- ally the police got the matter in hand, and from further enquiries which were made it is suppos-d that the two men passed along Academe-street, in- to scot land-road, and then naught tlie 1.50 train to Liverpool. How they mantic:el to travel thus far , with the till, without arousing suspicion is a per. plexing featura in the theory.

TOWN COUNCIL NOTES.

There was a larger attendance than usual of the " general public " at the meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening, and they were rewarded by seeing theCorincilfor rather a majority of it, perf,rm the interesting feat of " turning its back upon, itself." Rut before it accomplished that little achievement, the Town Clerk read a letter from the Rev. IY. W. Rgyberits, secretary of the Anti-Gambling Cuamittetr of the Warrington Social Questions Union, The letter called the attention of the Council to the "alarm- ing prevalence of gambling amongst the population of the towie.'' After esiornerating several of the evils complained of,. a hope was expressed that the Mayor" sill see the necessity of at ()ace taking such action as will prevent existing laws remaining any longer a dead letter is this town." The Mayor suggestedthat the letter shoald be con- signed to the Watch Committee, and—after Alder- man Chandley had. interpolated the otwervation that steps had already beeit taken, to a very great extent, to obviate the evil,, and Councillor Fleming had been ruled out of order for seeking to " follow on "—the Mayor's suggestion was agreed to.

The Watch Committee's proceedings, an far as public record in the shape of minutes shows, seem to consist pretty exclusively of passing accounts. and these minutes were soon. disposed of. Then ,latrie the edifying display of how Conservative Town Councillors can, under the crack of the Party whip,vote for a thing at one meeting and against it at the next.Tbe Council at the previous meeting heed decided that a professional auditor of the Corpora- tion accounts should be appointed, and so unani- mously were they in favour of this that an amend- ment to the contrary effect, which was moved by Councillor Canned, did not even find any one to second it. On Tuesday night, the Finance Com- mittee proposed that Counciilor J. Shaw Green be appointed the auditor, and at once a strong opposi- tion was developed. Councillors Connell, :inlet- I hurst, and Muir, all declared themselves now as strongly opposed to the creation of the of of professional auditor. Alderman Blackhurst de- fended the proposal of the Coin Witter, and pointed out how completely the Council wouid stultify itself by going back on its previous resolutions. Coun- ci:lor Fleming remarked that some of those who were now opposing,originally approved of the appointment. Councillor James Funclough "did not like the way the appointment had been made," and he alleged that the proposal for appointing a professional auditor had come from Mr. Shaw Green himself, but the assertion was at once contradicted by Alderman Holmes, the chairman of the Finance Committee. Councillor Dixon seemed disposed to reiterate the same offensive imputation, but this only elicited a still more emphatic repudiation from the chairman of the Finance Committee. Councillor James Fairclough did not improve his position by the asser- tion : But you first got the notion from Mr. Shaw Green," for the alderman at once responded with a decided " Certainly not. A little later on, the Mayor himself volunteered the information that Alderman Holmes had, some time dgo, spoken to him of the desirability of appointing a profes- sional auditor.

Councillor Roberts made a forcible speech in defence of the proposal of the Committee, end Councillor Muir, for once, sat very uneasily under his trenchant criticisms. Mr. Roberts contended that Councillor Muir's opposi- tion might not be altogether disinterested seeing that the worthy Councillor is " Mayor's auditors," and came in for a proportion of the ninety odd pounds which are at present paid to the elective auditor. Mr. Muir, judging from his inter- ruptive remarks, denied that be took any share or these fees, but Mr. Roberts maintained that he " could quite understand the opposition of Council- lor Muir." Mr. Roberts further pointekl out that the gentlemen who were now posing a 4 economists Lad raised no voice against the proposal to appoint an auditor. "J f the Finance Committee had selected somebody else that opposition would never have arisen,'' he asserted. The objections had no reference to Mr. Shaw Green's suitability or capability but to his politics. A vigorous denunciation of Councillor Dixon's free- dom in canvassing the character and motives of members and officials of the Corporation followed, and that gentleman was &scribed as the " great ' h imorist " of the Council, seeing that he claims to be consulted by manufacturers of the town on municipal affairs. Mr. Roberts concluded his speech by referring to the large properties now in the hands of the town, and the consequent need of an auditor.

Counciltor flepherd declared in favour of the amendment, and against the appointment of Mr. Green, while Alderman Ifarrison backed the Corn- mitttee and argued that the present elective auditors simply checked the accounts and did not audit them. Councillor R. T. Fairclough thought it would be better to go in for an Act of Parliament to get rid of the elective auditors before appoint- ing a professional auditor, and Councillor Bolton, ush's oszentatiously asserted that he had not at- tended any political meeting in regard to that sub• ject, said he would vote for the amendment because Mr. Green had not resigned hi a position as a town councillor. Alderman Uolmes described the dis- cu.sion as of the most extraordinary character, and he protested against the Council stultifying itself and the Finance Committee being made fools of. A vote was then taken, when the amendment rejecting the proposal of the Finance Committee was curled by fifteen votes to eleven.

In moving the minutes of he Health C.untnittee Alderman Sutton mentioned t hat for the last few weeks there had only been an average of three cases per week of smallpvx, and that there were no more than twonty-two cases in the Hope Hospital. With regard to the recent fire at the Aiken-st. hospital, there had been an inquiry, but as to the cause of t he confl igration there was a diversityof of,inion,and the Committee had not come to any decision upon the point. Upon another point, however, there was no doubt—the fire had cost the Corporation be- tween .E7OO and £BOO, because there had been no insurance. Councillor Bolton wanted to know why an insuranc had not been effected, and Cotincillor Plumpton maintained that the absence of insurance showed that things had been going on in a loose unbusinesslike way. Some debate also ensued as to the alleged refusal of the use of the %Vorkbouse hose. The lack of water at the hospi- tal for suppressing a fire was commented upon by Councillor Bonne!), and Councillor Beaman drew. comfort from the fact that the fire had afford° them some valuable experience against the time when they built another hospital. Councillor Dixon gently insinuated that the Chairmen of Com- mittees were responsible for the insurance, or non- insurance, of Corporation property, and Alderman Sutton wound up the discussion by saying that they had all succeeded in convincing themselves how clever they were, and how careful of the interests of their constituents. At the same time Ito re- gretted that some gentleman had not been a little more "previous" and told them about insurance before the hospital was burned down.

'rile Sanitary Works Committee proposed a re- arrangement of the duties or the inspectors, where- by Inspector Church was to be appointed sanitary works inspector and be ander the direction or Mr. Dees, manager of Longford depot, and not tinder that of Inspector Norman as heretofore. TESTIMONIAL TO MR. CHARLES ASHU RST.

A meeting of the Committee having charge of the te.timonial to Mr. Charles Ashurist, late station master at Bank Quay, Warrington, was held on Monday evening at the office of Mr. E. Braide, of the London and North Western Railway Company. Mr. Spencer Wellhead (treasurer) stated that the subscription amounted to £7B, and he requested Mr. Braide to make the presentation. Mr. Braide, after alluding to Mr. Ashurst's long services with the London and North Western Railway Company as station master at St. Helens and Warrington, and the efficient manner in which lie had always per- formed his many difficult duties to the satisfaction of the railway company and the nublic, presented Mr. Ash urst with a purse of £75 in gold, and a handsome barometer, bearing the following inscrip- tion : "This testimonial, with a purse of gold, sub- scribed by railway passenzers and employes, was presented to Mr. Charles Ashurst, on his retirement from the past of station master, Bank Quay, War- rington, May, 1893." Mr. Thomas Morris cordially endorsed all that Mr. Braide had FO well, said, and he also spoke very highly of Mr. Ashurat a close at- tention to his duties, and the satisfaction he had given to the railway company, and to all classes. Mr. Ashurst, after thanking Mr. Braids, Mr. Morris, and Mr Wellhead, expressed his deep gratitude to the subscribers, not only for the money so gener- ously given, but also for the many kind letter he had received.

WARRINGTON SOCIAL QUESTIONS UNION.

Since the first meeting held some weeks ago at the Museum, of the Social Questions Union for Warrington, a considerable amount of work has been done. Committees have been formed of mem- bers of various Christian communities in the town, for the purpose of combating the evils of gambling, drunkenness, social impurity, an also for the pur- pose of generally promoting the social and moral well-being of the community. The follow- ing are the officers of the various committees : Temperance Committee, chairman, Mr. E. J. Guest, J.P. vice-chairman, the Rev. E. T. Reed, secretary, Mr. S. B. Stringer Conditions of Hme Life, chairman, Mr. F. Monk, J.P., secretary, Mr. A. FL Lee; Labour Com- mittee, cliiirman, Dr. Starkey Smith, secretary, Mr. J. Haley ; Social Purity Committee, chairman, the Rev. E. T. Reed, secretary, Mr. Clement, George ; Gambling Committee, chairman, Rev. John Yonge, vice-chairman, Mr. John S. Green, secretary, the Rev. D. W. Roberts; Educational and Recreative Committee, chairman, Mr. C. 11. Kerry, secretary, the Rev. W. 11. Drummond. THINGS HEARD AND SEEN

AT WARRINGTON.

At the Tory meeting on Wednesday night, in the Conservative Club, the proceedings were a little " mixed" from some cause or other, ant anyone who went there for fan had it to their heart's content. To begin with, Alderman Harrison moved the e'er:- tion of Mrs.Reynolds,as lady ruling councillor of the habitation before he moved a vote of thanks for her services during the past year, Then Captain Buckton moved a vote of thanks to the officers of the past year. Councillor Muir was called upon to second the motion, and this was the fashion in which he performed the task : " Mrs. Reynolds, Ladies and Gentlemen, I rise to move (cries of second) 0, I have to second, but what have I to second, I don't know what is proplsed."Amid roars of laughter, the worthy councillor was enlightened on the matter. Mr.llarrison, acting for Mrs. Reynolds, then called upon Alderman Francomti to move a re- solution. Ile could not make out what that reso- lution was, but the programme seemed to read, " Alderman Francomb will now propose Mr. Dig "(loud !aughter). This strange resolution waQ, however, not proposed, as another resolution Was proposed by Alderman Francomb. and seconded by Mr. Diggle. I am very much startled to know that there is another political party in Warrington. We have already a Liberal party, a Tory Party, a Liberal Unionist party, au Indepv.ndent Labour party in embryo. but Mr. Diggle announced that he had been a " political party" for some years. Fortunately this is a party of oue, and has therefore very little weight. From Obi point to the end the meeting went on compara- tively smoothly, although a resolution was pro- posed and carried about an hour before Mr. It. Pier- rose to support it.

Before the Warrington magistrates, on Monday, John Bradbury, butcher, of 197, Winwick-road, was charged with exposing 57 pieces of beef and three pieces of mut- ton for sale, weighing 431b5., on the 29th ult.,which was unfit for sale. The Town Clerk said that on the 29th ult. Inspector Norman visited the premises of defendant and found the pieces of meat above stated to be in an ad- vanced state cf decomposition. The Inspector at once seized them and Dr. Gorrtall on examining them found them to be unfit for human food, and a magistrate's order was secured for their destruction. The meat was old meat, and had been kept from the previous Friday. Inspector Norman and Dr. Gornall corroborated. The Bench thought this case was more from carelessness than from a desire to break the law, and fined defendant £1 including costs.

A " SHARP CYCL/ST." At the Occasional Court, Warrington, on Tuesday morning, before Mr. Edward Greenall, John Mulvey was charged with stealing a bicycle, the property of Dr. S. Parker, Stockton Heath:—Dr. Parker stated that he left the bicycle at the door on Mmda,y evening, and on receiving a communication requiring his services in an amputation case at the Latchford Hospital, he tried to find his bicycle, but failed. He later received such in- formation that the prisoner's arrest was effected at Hill Cliff Chapel, where he was engaged as a plasterer.—Two witnesses stated that they saw the prisoner trying to ride the bicycle which they immediately identified as belong- ing to Dr. Parker.—The bicycle was found reared up against the wall of the prisoner's house.—Prisoner was remanded to the Daresbury Petty Sessions. Bail was granted in prisoner's own security of £2O and that of two sureties of £lO each. GIVING THE WRONG CHANGE. At the Warrington Borough Court, yesterday, John Fleming, a street loafer, was charged with obtaining a half sovereign, the property of William Ellison, by means of a trick on the previous day. Mr. Hunt (chief con- stable), said that Fleming was at the Blue Bell Inn on Thursday afternoon, and he was requested by a lady to bring her a pound of strawberries from the market. She gave him is., and there would therefore be 6d. change. Prisoner went to the market to Mrs. Massey's stall, and asked complainant who is employed at the stall, for the strawberries. Complainant served him, but in giving him the change, he gave him a half sovereign in mis- take for a sixpence. Finding out his mistake soon afterwards, complainant went after him, and found him coming out of a public house. Instead of keeping him in sight, Ellison went in search of a police - man, and gave a description of him. P.S. ravlor ar- rested prisoner in the afternoon. Prisoner was sent to gao! for three months.

THEFTS FEOM THE MARKET. Mary Wood, 2,Dohnan's-lane, was charged at Warring- ton Police Court on Thursday, with stealing from the Market Place, two silk handkerchiefs, the property of Mr. A. Peacock, and a pair of stockings the property of Thomas West, on Wednesday. Prisoner.pleaded guilty. The Chief Constable said the complainant Arthur Pea- cock lived at 152, Winwick-road, and kept a stall in the Market. Between five and six o'clock prisoner was seen by the complainant loitering about his stall, and the prisoner was carrying a child on her arm, with an elder child by her side. The complainant watched her from a distanse and saw her take the two silk handkerchiefs, and put them underneath the clothes of the child she was carrying. In doing so a corner of one of the hand- kerchiefs was hanging out, and the elder child very adroitly pushed it under. She was going away when he stopped her, and gave her into the custody of Inspector Spinks. A pair of stockings was also found on the child and it was found that they had been taken from the stall of Mr. T. West, residing at 5, Legh-st. When charged with stealing them she said" I didn't steal them the little child picked them off the floor." She was sent t& gaol for N days hard labour. INFERIOR MILK ON SALE.
