Smith said that ' he was near Wash-lane on the day in question, when he heard two horses and traps coming at a furious rate down Black Bear Bridge. They passed him at such a speed that he had great difficulty in identifying them. They were having a race, and were probably going at the rate of 15 or 16 miles an hour. P.C. Corcoran cor- roborated, and Benjamin Milliechap said that a bicyclist bad a narrow escape of being run over. Ramsbottom said he was agreeably surprised and very proud to know that he had a horse that could go at such a rate as the officer had stated. He was not previously aware of the fact. The bench were determined to make an example of the case and inflicted a penalty of 40s. and costs on each defendant. _ _

James Ramsey, of the Sandon Hotel, Liverpool, was granted permission to sell on a field at Sutton VI eaver from 12 noon to 8 p.m. on Saturday the 24th June on the .occa- sion of the London And North-Western Railway sports. 1 SIIOCICING FAMILY DISPUTE —Margaret Rushton, of Woods Row, Halton-road, was summoned for using threats towards Margaret Setfon, her sister, of York-st., on the 22nd May. The defendant was further charged with assaulting Mrs. Sefton on the 29th May.—Mr: Burton appeared for the complainant. On the first occasion the defendant threatened to Mt her face off with a broken tile. On Monday, the case came up at Court, but was adjourned, and the defendant at once went to complainant's house, burst open the back door and assaulted her, alsw dragging her by the hair half 1 way up the yard. It transpired during the case that the trouble arose through the defendant believing that the complainant harboured her husband at her house.—The defendant contended that it was a "fair 'fight." Her children were wanting bread and that was sufficient reason for what she had done.—Superintendent Ather- ton said the defendant was a terror to the locality.—She was ordered to be bound over to keep the peace for six 'months, and pay the costs 11s. 61. in the first ease, and ' for the assault she was fined ss. and costa ho. 61' The defendant said she would go to gaol before she would pay a halfpenny.—A summons was then heard in which Mrs. Rushton, charged her husband William Rushton with deserting her and neglecting to maintain her. She stated that lie left her on the 13t11 March and she "never heard tell of him" until three weeks ago. He had never' contributed a halfpenny to her since. He was a labourer at Mathieson's and earned 30e. a week. She had five of a family, three of whom were under seven years of age. —ln cross-examination by Mr. Burton, complainant denied that there had been any trouble about a lodger, whom Mr. Burton said had more to do with the man- agement of the house than the husband had.—lt was stated that the lodger was now living with the woman, and that she had refused her husband admittance to the house.—The Bench made an order upon the defendant to contribute 10s. per week towards the support of his wife and family. "THE CLEVEREST BEGGAR IN BATTE R- SEA. " A officer of a local bratlelt of the Charity Organization Society has discovered a family who are known to their neighbours as" the cleverest beggars in Battersea." The title appears to have been honestly,or rather dishonestly earned. Some two years ago this official's opinion was asked by a medical man regarding a family in that part who seemed to be in great distress. The home was a miserable one, the children dirty and untidy, the father appeared to be always out of work, and it was difficult to see how the family managed to ke2p out of the work- house. It was found, however, that at one and the same time they were in receipt Gf out-door relief from the Guardians, and were getting help from the clergy- man of the parish in which they lived, as well as from a large Dissenting chapel in the same district The clever family were also being assisted by the Salvation Army and by members of the Church Army, whose meetings the man attended in a parish quite a mile away from his own home. Not long after this they left the district, but very soon news of them was received from another relief committee, to whom the man seems to have represented himself as" an infidel lately won over to Christianity."

LANCASHIRE, DERBYSHIRE, AND EAST COAST RAILWAY.

The Lancashire, Derbyshire, and East Coast Railway Bill, which has already passed the House of Lords, catne on Thursday before the examiner of the House of Com- mons. The question of the compliance of this Bill with Standing Orders having been inquired into,the examiner decided to allow the Bill to proceed to its second reading in the House of Commons.

THE LlisE RATOR FRAUDS.

" SHOCKING ! PERFECTLY SHOCKING."

The Liberator Society's affairs were further investigated in London on Tuesday. Mr. Morrell Theobald was called, and stated that he raised objections on certain points connected with the Liberator Society, with the result, that he was cold-shouldered by the entire board of directors. Beyond what had been stated by his brother as having been received from the Liberator, namely £3,500 odd, he had not himself received a penny. Mr. Leonard Burns was called.

Mr. J. 11. Pattison said he had been a director of the whole of the Balfour comnanieq, but it seemed incredi- ble that he had received £20,000 in fees.

Mr. Wheeler: You may take it from me that it is so. Mr. Wheeler read a resolution which was passed upon the removal of the Liberator into its new offices :—" That, on the occasion of taking possession this day of our new premises, the directors desire to record their sense of thankfulness. for the prosperity with which, as they believe, God his hitherto blessed their efforts in the establishment of this business." (," Oh, oh," and laughter). The Registrar : Who seconded it ? Mr. Wheeler : The Rev. D. Burns.

The Registrar : Shocking, ! perfectly shocking ! Continuing witness said he had made many speeches at meetings of the society, but did not claim to be its orator.

ASLEEP IN CHARGE OF A HORSE AND CART.—Thomas Gleave, was charged with being asleep while in charge of a horse and cart in Sankey, on the 18th inst. P.C. Dugdale proved the case, and Mrs. Gleave who appeared for her husband, stated that he had had no sleep for two nights previously.—Defendaat was ordered to pay the costs.

- GIVIN6 Floa " Rom', RULE"—Patrick Loyden was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Croft on the 21st ult. Defendant created much amusement by ad- dressing the magistrates as " Your honours," and your lordships," and said that another man ought, to have been summoned, as he kept tinocking his hat off, and saying " I'll give you Rome Rule." Defendant was fined 21, 81. and costs.

ASSAULT ON A CONSTABLE.—Henry Smith, Iship canal labourer. was charged with being drunk and disorderly, and also with assaulting P.C. Little at Rixton while in the execution of his duty, on May 30th. Complainant said that prisoner and a large number of people had just come out of the Bowling Green Inn, Rixton, at closing time on the 30th ult.' and as they were drunk. witness and P.C. Pope told them to go home. Prisoner upon this turned round and struck witness in the face They then, fell to the ground together, and prisoner kicked hit ia in the stomach to such an extent that wit- ness had been off duty ever since. In the meantime P.C. Pope had been attacked, and a man he bad ar- rested had been rescued by.. the others, and Pope then came to witness's assistance and prisoner was locked up. Prisoner was fined 40s. and costs or a months' hard labour.

WARRINGTON V. WIGAN.--rheso teams met fdr the first time on Saturday in miserable weather, at Wigan. Warrington lacked the services of their captain, Mr. R. Smith. A start was made at 2.15, with Mr. J. J. Cros- field and Sugg opening to the deliveries of Walton and W. F. Goodwin. The light was very bad, ,and in the professional's second over, Sugg was taken splendidly at short leg, the visiting professional having failed to score. Alin then began to fall heavily, and accompanied by thunder and lightning, the probability of any wore play looked remote. However, after threequarters of all hour, the game was resumed, Mr. S. M. Crosfield accom- panying his cousin to the wickets, despite the fact that the ground was-in certain places covered with water. Runcama steadily, but at eleven the initial batsman was completely beaten, and G. Clarke joined the visiting skipper. At 42, Clarke, in hits eagerness to notch a short run, failed to draw Mr. S. M. Crosfield, and a good wicket was thus literally thrown away. Crosfield played on without any addition to the score, whilst at 43 Clark was caught by Halliwell—five wickets being down. Reynolds played patiently and Podmore early showed an inclination to, hit, sending Walton to the boundary, but at 48 the last comer was taken by Halliwell, whilst with an additional run Reynolds was run out. Bell was beaten by Goodwin after making five, but trouble was given' by Bolton and Griffin. Runs came but slowly, chiefly by the aid of Bolton, until at 69 Griffin placed one in the hands of mid-on, and the venture closed for 61. Wigan came out of the ordeal with 96 runs. 223 LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE ASSOCIATION.

WARRINGTON V. LRlGH.—Representative teams of these districts met on the green at the Ship Hotel, Irlam, on Thursday to play in the second series of champion- ship competitions. Warrington joined the County As- sociation last year, and succeeded in taking the premier prize in the second division. Leigh have been affiliated for three seasons, and were thrown out of the champion- ship division last season by a couple of paints only. The first win was secured by Warrington, but after- wards Leigh pulled ahead, and making allowanco for a heavy adverse return at the close of the seventh round, continued to increase their majority until he close of the event. Score : LEIGH,

WARTV.NGTON. J Grundy J Isherwood J Hill JH Cocker 2l W Littlewood W Cocker H Hinclley 2l NV Bolton H Forshaw W Prescott . 2l T Walsh s T Smith J Southern 2l J Nevins W Houwhton 2l T Unsworth ' 12 WEatCliffe 2l A J Jones

227 lE aj orl ty 42 for Leigh. PRINCESS MAY'S WEDDING MiESSEN,

Princess May's wedding dress and those of the brides- maids arc being prepared by Miss Linton,who has made gowns for Princess May and her Royal cousins since they were little girls, The Materials are to be exclusively English, and even the trimmings are selected with a view to benefiting British trade as much as possible. The looms at Spitalfields are busily employed on silks and brocades for the trousseau. Lace-makers about Exeter and in Buckinghamshire are producing their filmy fabrics with skilful fingers, the Duchess of Teck having chosen designs and given orders. West of Eng- land serges and woollens are also requisitioned, nor are Irish poplins and even friezes ignored. Linens from Belfast, and lace from Limerick, are included in the orders issued. Even far Shetland feels the wave that has broken over Great Britain, giving an impetus to trade which was badly needed. The stony island of , Harris supplies a blue tweed for the Princess's wear,and Ross-shire has also produced two at least of the trous- seau dresses. Straw from Luton,ribbons from Coventry, and embroideries from the skilful cottage workers in the North of Ireland are gll being collected and wrought up into fashionable wear. .It is hoped that the example thus set of encouraging British trade may possibly be followed by English ladies in general.

SIXPENCE FOR A SHEEP.

A FINE OF £5O.

At Guildhall (London) Police Court, on Wednesday, Albert E. Barnwell, a butcher, living at Finedon. near Wellingborough, was charged with having sent to the Central Meat Market the carcase of a sheep which was deceased and totally unfit for human food. Mr. Vickery, who prosecuted on behalf of the Commissioners of Sewers, and said this about the worst case that had ever come before a court of justice. Evidence would show that it was dressed as a lamb, that it was in a filthy condi- tion, the weight being 24lbs. whereas were it heathy it should be 56Ibs. The defendant, being a butcher, should have known its condition, and no doubt did know as when he sold the skin, &c., the sheep cost only 6d. Dr, sedgwick Saunders said lie had examined the meat. The animal had suffered from a wasting disease, and it was the worst case of the kind he had ever seen. Eating such stuff might be attended with serious results. Mr. Alderman Moore hesitated whether he should not send the defendant to gaol without giving him the caption of fine. He had,however, previously held a good character, and taking that into consideration, the alderman said he would give him the option of a fine, but it would be the maximum one of £5O, and £2 2s. costs.

THE PENKETII WATER SUPPLY.

RUNCORN.

MR. W. DIGGLE TO THE RESCUE.

Some of the Penketh inhabitants have been dis- satisfied with their water supply of late, and in consequence of their complaints, the Rural Sanitary Authority have been in negociation with Warring- ton and Liverpool, in order to obtain an efficient supply for the village. Mr. Wm. Diggle'a philanthropic heart, for to use his own words, be is a philanthropist, I was stirred, when he heard of the poor sanitary condi- tion of the place, of which he claims to be a "half-bred." He accordingly called a meeting on Monday night a'. the Penketh Conservative Club, at his own expense, to advise the villagers is to the water they should choose. Toward 7.30, he was to be seen coming along the road attired in a white waistcoat, and with a red rose in his coat. On coming to the end of Stock's-lane, he invited a good number of people who were waiting for him, to step into the Conservative Club in order to view the different waters, of which he had specimens. In this room, there wasa formidable array of bottles containing the specimens of water, glasses, and piping in which the lime of the Warrington water had formed a thiek cake. The room quickly filled, and Mr.Diggle announced that the first business would be to elect a chairman, and Mr. Redmond was chosen for that position. The chairman very briefly opened the proceedings and then called upon Mr. Diggle to give his lecture. Mr. Diggle, who was received with applause, said that he had had large experience during the last 50 years on the subject of" Water." He bad lost money by bad water, and ga'ned by good water. It was likely that the inhabitants of Penketh vi.otild have watet forced upon them, and it was his object that night to advise them as to the best water, as they would have to pay the same for bad as good water. They might perhaps want to know why he appeared there that night. Well, he was a philanthropist, and his mother came from Pen- keth so that he was a sort of "half-bred "inhabitant of the village. He would now proceed to test several waters he had brought with him. Mr. Diggle amid much amusement proceeded to pour out a glassful each of Vyrnwy, Appleton, Winwick, Manchester, Widnes, and Penlceth waters, and tested each of them with a tea spoonful of lime water, and gave a very favourable opinion of Vyrnwy and Manchester water, but an un- favourable one of the others. He argued that water should be fit both for domestic and manufacturing pur- poses. The Liverpool supply was superior in this respect to the Warrington as there was less lime in it. Water with lime in solution, left a cake of lime in the piping, when it was heated, and in addition to making the tube narrower it was a bad conductor of heat, and thus caused more fuel to be used. He would advise them, therefore, to have the Vyrnwy water. . _ .

Mr. Cross said he did not think that the Penketh water did the inhabitants much harm, as the death-rate was lower than in most rural areas, and they wanted water for domestic purposes and not for manufacturing purposes, to which Mr. Diggle rejoined that it would be better if they could get it for both. Mr. Diggle pro- ceeded amid many interruptions, and then asked if any of the audience had anything more to say. Mr. C. E. PARKER said he was much obliged to Mr. niggle for calling the meeting. He (the speaker) was mainly responsible for forcing the question to the front, and whatever blame or praise was due, would fall upon him. When the wells were near sinks, &c., they werc. always in danger of b ing contaminated, and the watei for drinking purposes was far from good. The was would always be in danger where cottages were thickly gathetid together. Coming to the manufacturers point of view, he did not wish to unduly influence those who had shares in the tannery. But the village depended a great deal upon it, directly or indirectly. Unless the tanner, could get a sufficient supply of water for manu- facturing purposes, they would have to go elsewhere. They had no other course and in fact they had been in negociations about some property near Liverpool. He thought that should have some weight. Some of those present seemed to think that the matter was only important as far as domestic purposes were concerned, but he thought that the interests of manufacturers ought to have some weight also. (Hear, hear.) He proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. niggle. Mr. HOBSON seconded the motion, and it was passed unanimously. Mr. Diggle replied. Mr. Cross proposed that Mr. Parker call a township meeting to be held shortly. Mr.Hobion seconded and it was passed unanimously. Mrs. Garnett having briefly expressd her pleasure at hearing Mr. Diggle, the meeting terminated.

Mr., George Price, Bañkof

land, St. Simon St., Salford, says :

"For many years I have been afflcted with most excruciating pains in my limbs, which at times drove me most mad, incapacitating me from attending to business for weeks at a time. After a few applications of ST. JACOBS OIL the change in me is simply marvellous—l am a new man. I have spent scores of pounds in doctors' medicines, but all have proved futile. I give you this inform

mation for the interests of my fellow men, and you have my permission to make whatever use of same you think best."

IRON PROM

The extraction of metal from the magnetic ironsands found on certain portions of the-Pacific North American coast seems to be making progress. We learn that a plant is being erected on the shore at Crescent City,Cali- fornia, whizti it is expected will obtain daily 100 tons of iron from 1,000 tons of arenacious matter treated, the metal to be shipped to San Francisco for further manipu- L.Ation. The operations are to be Cellehleted under the etiportgiop of a Mr. Arkew, who has patented an electri- cal ppyoess or maneacturing iron from black sand, and whose Set-Vices have been engaged by a convention known as the Lawson-Rico Syndicate..—lron.

THREATENED WATER FAMINE MANCHESTER.

At the meeting of the Manchester City Council, on Wednesday,SirJ. T. flarwood stated that there was only seventy days' supply in the reservoirs, and unless rain came copiously, the supply to the city would have to be curtailed. The Corporation had not gone to Thirlmere for water a moment too soon. They might get some Thirlmere water soon, but not within seventy days. It was worthy of note that within a year works whic.h had cost Manchester £20,000,000 sterling would be opened. He considered the tact unpredecented.

===== 0002601_18930610_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GOVERNMENT AND THE UI lONISTS.

The "Belfast Telegraph" on Thursday states that the Government, having become alarmed at the position oi affairs in the North of Ireland, have directed the county inspectors throughout Ulster to inquire as to the objects of the Ulster Defence Union, and the number and status of the persons enrolled. The Irish Executive are also seriously considering the advisability of proclaiming the proposed Ulster assembly of 800, which will meet about the 15th of next month, as being an illegal organisation under the Convention (Ireland) Act, 1879.

===== 0002601_18930610_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DROUGHT IN NORM WALES.

In parts of North Wales, on Wednesday, there were severe local thunderstorms, but, speaking generally, the drought continues with even greater severity. The mountains streams have all dried up, and the Dula, which washed away the railway bridge on the Holyhead line near Llandulas, is now a mere gutter, with pools hero and there. The prospects for the hay harvest are the most dismal for the past ten years.

WAVERING HOME RULERS.

I The " Globe "is informed that Sir Edward Reed (L) and Mr. T. H. Bolton (L) have both received satisfac- tory assurances from the Opposition that hi the event of their finally deciding to resist the Home Rule Bill, they will not be opposed in their constituencies by any Union- ist. Mr. Bolton, in particular, has been assured that his late opponent, Mr. Moon, will not only refrain from opposing him, but will assist him, should his present attitude be maintained.

FREE EDUCATION.

WHAT IT IS AND HOW TO GET IT:

• The fo;lowing memorandum has been issued by the Committee of Council on Education : Crider the Education Act of 1891 the greater numbe- Gf the elementary schools in England and Wales are now free. With regard to districts where all school places are net yet free, the following memorandum explains the rights of parents, and how they may obtain tree educed on for their children. 1. ilielit of all Parents to Free Education.—Every father and Mother in England and Wales il!IS a right Co free education, without payment Or charge of any kind, for his or her Children between the age of three and fifteen. The right ,to free education is not a concession to poverty, but is common to all classes alike. Any parent who ha's not got free education already may write to the Education Department and claim it, either alone or in combination with other parents. 2. Form of Claim for Free Education.—The claim may be made in the blinwing form of words :—" I (or wej desire free educatien for . the child (or children) men- tioned below, and I (or we) represent te the Department that there is at present insufficient free school accommodation in the district for suds child (or children)." 3. What Free Education Meins.—The Free Education 0 which parents have a right must be unconditionel— that is to say, must not be free while the child is in certain standards only, or be given on the ground of poverty, or be subject to any inquiry as to the means of the parent or the reasons the parents has for desiring it, or be free only on condition that the child attends regul-_ arty, er have any other condition attached to it. It must be wholly free, without any charge for books, slates, or anything else ; and it must be At a fichool within a reasonable distance of the child's home. 4. flow Free Education is Provided.—When the .Education Department receive a claim for free educa- tion on behalf of one or more children, they make inquiry IS to what free school places are available for these children, and if the number of free places is insufficient, cause a sufficient number to be provided. In districts where there is a School Board, the School Board is dir- ected to previde them ; in districts where there is no School 'Board, if the free places recmired are not pre; vided by voluntary schools; a School Board is formed, Ind directed to provide them in like manner. . - 6. Children not to Cease Attending School while Waiting for :Free Places:—Parents who have claimed tree education, but have not vet obtained it, must go on ! sendine their childien to school, even if this inv.lves paying fees, until the free places have been provided in the way explsiued above. 8. Charges fer Books and Stationery.—The managers of all schools, whether they are free schools or not, are bound to provide a p :*oper supply of books, slates, and other echool apparatus, and cannot compel a parent to provide books either by periodical payment or by pur- etiase, nor can they refuse admission to a child whose parent refuses to buy them. But if a parent prefers to buy school books outright, so that they may remain the property of the child, there is nothing to prevent his buying them from the managers, or the managers selling them to him, as a purely voluntary arrangement and a matter of mutual convenience.

GHASTLY DISCOVERY IN A CLERGY- MAN'S GARDEN.

On Wednesday afternoon, a startling discovery was made at Douglas, in the garden of the Rev. W. T. Hob- son vicar of St.. Barnabas's. It appears that a woman, name] Ellen Flynn aged 30, domestic servant at the Athol Hotel, was observed about noon to be going to- wards the garden in question with a parcel under her arm. Her movements were watched, and in a few minutes she returned minus the parcel. She had thrown it over a hedge some Bit. high. A person accosted her,atid asked her what the parcel contained. He did not get any satisfactory reply, and information was given to the police, who went into the garden, and found the body of a newly-born child, about a day old, wrapped in &towel and apron, with a newspaper round it. The woman was traced to the Athol Hotel, where she WAS taken into custody and charged with the offenceovhich she admitted at once. It transpired that the child was born about three o'clock on Tuesday morning. The ser- vant had been asking for have, but no suspicion was aroused. The woman was in a weak conditioa, and was sent to the hospital. The affair has created quite a sensation.

===== 0002601_18930610_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
MIL LABOUCHERE AND THE HOUSE

OF LORDS.

Mr. H. Lahouchere, M.P., speaking at Northampton on Wednesday night, explained the purport of his two Bills relating to the House of Lords. BY the first he proposes that no man should be at the head of a Govern- ment department who was not a member of the House of Commons. He would give peers the chance of election to the House of Commons, but deprive them of their title. His other Bill was to provide that in January, 1895, the House of Lords shall cease to exist. 'Though in favour only of bile Chamber, Iv., would consent to two elected Chambers. Be condemned the Unionist policy regarding the Home Rule Bill as purdy obstructive, and said a nu et ing of Itadial mernb-trs, adjourned to Friday contemplated asking Mr. Gladstone th take very driS7ic action. He thought Mr. Gladstone took the Opposition athendments too seriously. He did not realise the iniquities of their opponents, and he threw his pearls belore these swine. He must treat thein with silent contempt if, this Bill was to be carried. Unless this obstruction was put down he was afraid they would 1 )se seats in the country next election.

FAGTOItY'LEGISLATION FOR INDIA.

Considerable progress was made with the Employers' Liability Bill before the Standing Committee on Law on rhursday. Mr. John Burns pointed out that there were ! in this county and in Yorkshire gres.t complaints as CO the difficulty of competing with India in the cotton,jute and textile trades. The law and trade unions had im- posed shorter hours higher wages, Factory Acts, Sanita- tion,and employers'liability. The operatives in India had not the advantage of working under these changed condi- tions; but as India was a dependency of the :British Crown it was thought the bill, so far as the textile trades were concerded, should apply to them. In fact. the masters and the hands in the c)tton trade of this coun- try maintained that the conditions imposed on their in- (leery should be applied to the same industry in India. lie expressed his intention of moving a clause on the subject at the Committee stage. Several amendments were adopted and the scope of the bill extended to Scot- land. The Committee again adjourned.

• WARRINGTON SOCIAL QUESTIONS UNION. Sir,—The attention of this Union has been called, in one of its Committees, to a practice that is said to pre- vail in public-houses in this town, which, if it really is in existence, needs to b! put down speedily, and with a resolute hand. We are informed that it is a custom with many pub- licans to encourage the fetching of drink by children for nonsumption try others, by giving the children "sweets." Also, that with some publicans it is a further custom to , increase this encouragement by the offer of an additional I bribe, that if so many pints or quarts are fetched by one child, a threepenny piece is dropped into the last mea- sure carried out, presumably as a reward for the child, but which may more correctly be presumed is a tip for the drinker. It is said that the landlady of one house has expressed a hope that the former practice is not illegal, in isnauch as she has recently laid in a considerable stock sweets. It is our strong opinion, which we doubt not will be shared by many of your readers, that this practice, if it exist, should be put an end to. And we venture thus, with your kind permission, to call public attention to it. May I also add a very earnest appeal to parents net to send their children to fetch liquor from public-houses ? Surely it will occur to them that the sights and sounds which are characteristic of the public-house cannot be desirable for the children to be farniliariud with. If therefore the use of alcoholic drinks is deemed neces- sary, may I suggest that the parents themselves person- ally procure them ? They will thus save the children from growing up in touch with the public-house, and may help to prevent unhappy consequences to the chil- dren both now in their childhood, and afterwards in their mature years.—l am, yours faithfully, JOUN YONGE, President of the above Union. 19, Wilson Patten-st., June 6, 1893.

===== 0002601_18930610_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
GAMBLING ON ADULT AND CHILDREN'S LIVES.

Sir,—As there is no one to accept my challenge to dis- cuss this question of gambling, I am debarred from bringing to view, those inconsistencies which I am sure are practised to an enormous extent, and Ido not pro- pose to occupy your valuable space week after week. I would like to have given your readers some of the information I possess, gained from practical experience el over four years' service as canvasser, agent,assista.nt, superintendent, and district superintendent. The glar- ing evils that exist take sway much of the philanthro- pic element, and the cultivation of debit, bonuses,divi- dends, &c., takes a leading part in the business, so mat- ter how the object is attained. . . . . . . .

One great objection is the unfair and unlawful insur- ing millions of persons without signatures, whereby the husband can be entered without his consent in almost every office. Every relative, however distant, is thrust in these contracts without consent. Children are made objects of insurance by simply getting the parents' con- sent, or wi ;Mut their consent when no signatures are required. The books of re,;istrars will show vast num- bers of second and third certificates, where children have been entered on one, two, and even the third policy. What says a celebrated authority on these questions : —" We do net believe that people generally kill their ckildren, or allow them to die tor insurance money, but there is not the least doubt that the delicate in many instances are entered for the highest sums possible ; amounts that can never be met out of premiums paid in the infantile department. lhe poor little creatures should never have been entered at all,misch less for such ' heavy sums. We suffer much loss through the reckless scramble for this kind of business. The numerous very early claims that have been and are still being made (under policies of less than one year's duration) is really amazing. Thistwe consider, goes to prove that there is a widespread disposition to gamble and speculate on ha- man life."

I cannot quote a higher authority, nor yet can stronger words be expressed. There is no one who dares to meet them. But I must mention a fact not generally known, namely, that agents and superintendents have been, and are so eager for business that they actually eontract for, and make the bargain before the child is born, and should the child die before three months, the agents and superintendents alike will make up a grant of ss. to 10s. out of their wages, providing the office does not do it for them. I have seen too much of this sort of thing done. If cases could be given to illustrate some of the practices I have seen enacted, your readers would blush.—Yours faithfully.

===== 0002601_18930610_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
JOHN MOORP lELD. 44, rarker-st., Warrington, June 8, 1893. OUTDOOR VERSUS INDOOR RELIEF.

Sir,—lt may appear odd, that ene who is not a rate- payer in tho IVarrington Union should write to you on such a subject as the administration of the poor law in that district, but as I am myself a Guardian in one of the poorest unions in London, I always take any eppor- tuni'y that occurs of visiting the workhouses in the different parts of the country to pick up any improve-, ments that can be carried out with advantage. Under tiese circumstances, one gets, naturally, into the habit of comparing the work of other Boards with that of the one with which one is connected,and it is with the hope that I may stir up the ratepayers in the Warrington Union to take more care of their own interests and their own pockets, that I venture to send you this letter. The population of the union with which I am con- nected, is rather over 120,000, and that of Warrington I s I believe, about 60,000; at any rate, it may be taken to be about one half. In addition to this, the difference between the two unions in respect of wealth, is very great. In my own union, almost the entire population belongs to the working-class ; there are a few small shop- keepers, and a good many large furniture warehouses, a few saw mills, and two or three large factories. The houses in almost all the streets are let off as lodgings,two or three families living in one house, the rent of a bare room varying between 2.6 d. an 4 3s. 6d. a week. Sow, contrast this with the state of the Warrington Union. Here there are plenty of large works, where employment can be obtained; if you walk through the streets they always give the appearance of plenty of busi- ness going on ; the shops are large and look well-to-do, and certainly do not give the impression that it is a struggle for their owners to keep their heads above water ; but besides all this, the union contains many families who may be classed as rich, of which not a single specimen exists in the union with which I have to do.

Now, supposing for a moment, that the administration of out-door relief is the same in both Unions—and it should be remembered that all unions are in this respect subject to the same regulations—and taking into consid- eration the enormous difference in the populatim and also the difference in its character, it would naturally occur to most people, that the amount of out-door relief given in Warrington, would be somewhere about ens third of that given in the London Union. Let us turn to actual facts. During the week ending May 27th, 1893, the amount of out-door relict given in the London Union was £33 6s. 4d., while during the same week in Warrington, the amount given was £64 is. 111, or nearly double; the comparison between the two unions would appear to show that the amount which was necessary would be abcut £l2 It woul,i, therefore, seem that the Guardians of tha Watrington UlliQn are givoni In out-door relief, about fivo times as much as is required. Now such a startling difference as this, can only arise in one way, and that is that the Guardians of the War- rington Union pursue a different policy from that of the London Union, and the question arises which of these two policies is the beat. „ .

On this point, I would first quote the opinion of the Poor Law Commissioners, and then give a few instances of what has occurred in actual practice. In their report of 18,59 the Commissioners stated as follows :—(1) Destitution, as distinguished from poverty, alone consti- tutes a claim to Poor Law Relief. (2) The fundamental principle with respect to legal relief is, that the condition of the pauper ought to be, on the whole, less eligible than that of the independent labourer. (3) That, as a matter of practice, except in cases of urgent necessity, all distribution of relief, in money or in goods, to be spent or consumed by the pauper in his own horse, is inconsistent with the principle in question. (4) The only sure method of ascertaining whether an applicant is destitute, is to offer, in lieu of his present condition. an adequate but less eligible maintenance, which will not he accepted unless necessity requires it. This can be affected by the offer of the workhouse and by that only. Now I am quite aware that it is the habit of many Boards of Guardians to say tint this rule is too hard, and that applicants for relief ought not to be forced to go into the workhouse, but in practice I have found that a steady and consistent offer of the house to those ap- plicants for out-door relief, who can reasonably be supposed to be capable of earning something, does not force them in, but on the contrary forces upon them the necessity of maintaining themselves, instead of living at the expense of the ratepayers. The difficulty of carry- ing out this principle, is always greatest in those Unions where out-door relief has been given largely. The ap- plicant will say that some of his relatives, or his friends who are the same position as himself are in receipt of ut-door relief, and he will fight the Board to the last gasp sooner than give in and set to work, and these cases naturally require steady and consistent treatment. Boards of Guardians seem to forget that in spending other people's money they owe quite as much considera- tion to the poorer ratepayers, who are struggling to keep up a position of independence and who are liable to be driven over the line and become paupers if the rates they have to pay are unnecessarily high, as they owe to the man who comes to them for assistance.

Another point that is frequently lost sight of, if in- deed it is ever considered at all, is that to give ou.-door

relief largely is simply to increase the number of paurers. The children of tlicee wile are in receipt of reliet are simply bred up to expect it, and under the smallest strain will fall back upon the relieving officer, never having been taught to lay up something for a rainy day, whereas the right feeling to encourage should be that the applicant should recognize that it is a degrad- ing thing for him to be dependent on his neighbours and that he must make every endeavour by the exercise of thrift to prevent himself from falling into such a neees- city. I feel quite convinced that, if the recommenda- tions of the Poor Law Commissioners were honestly carried out, the ratepayers of Warrington would find that a considerable burden was taken off their shoulders; and, in addition to this, I maintain that it is their duty to elect as Guardians only those who by following such a policy would prevent that increase of , pauperism which is bound to occur where out-door relief is largely given, and which was nearly the ruin of the country previous to the Poor Law Reform of 1834. Let me now produce the testimony of two Unions, ' which have followed this policy, one a Metropolitan ' Union with which I was slightly connected some years , ago, the other an agricultural Union. A former reliev- ing officer of the Stepney Union gives his objections to 1 cutdoor relief as follows :-1. It discourages in them, the habits of thrift, sobriety, and frugality. 2. It destroys feelings of independence. 3. It blunts their natural energies. 4. It destroys sound v:ews of the duty of children to their parents ; and worse than that it destroys filial duty itself. 5. It encourages hypo- crisy and deceit. To this list, I would venture to add one more, viz., that it lowers the rate of waees, for it is evident that a man who is in receipt of 2s. 61. a week from the parish can afford to sell his labour cheaper,than a man who is receiving nothing, and the independent labourer is thus taxed to support the man who is taking the bread out of his mouth.

The Stepney Union have therefore practically abol- ished out-door relief with the following results as shown in the annexed table.

Half Year ending Half Yearly cost of Half yearly cost of Lady Day indoor-maintenance out-door relief R £ 1869 le7o 1871 1875 1879 1883 1886 1887

These include the cost of maintenance in Metropolitan Asylums, Board Hospitals (about £850) which thereto- fore had not been included.

These figures speak volumes, the ratepayers of the union have been practically saved £ll,OOO a year, and the cost of the maintenance of the indoor poor has also fallen, while the population of the district shows ne signs of falling off. Now consider the figures of a county Union. These figeres are taken from Bradfield. Population in 1871, 15,853 ; in 1881, 17,972. Years. In main- Out main- tenance texance. e £ d. 1871 1873 1875 1877 1879 1881 1883 1885 1887 65. 1888 In addition to this, between 1871 and 1888, subscrip- tions to medical clubs and Friendly Societies increased about 150 per cent. Now, I should like to know if anything can be con- sidered more satisfactory than this. Any Union can carry it out if only the Guardians will use their powers rightly, and there is no r,:asen why the ratepayers of Warrington should have to pay £3,300 a year, the greater part of which goes to enable the idle and im- provident to live at the expense of their industrious neighbours.—l am, yours faithfully, A LONDON GUARDIAN.

WALKING DA.Y AT WARRINGTON. RUMOURED POSTPONEMENT.

It is generally iumoured about the town that an attempt is going to be made to postpone Walking Day from the 30th June to the 6th of July, the date of the Royal Wedding. It is also understood that the question will be brought up at the public town's meeting, to be held on Monday night for the purpose of deciding whether the Ot h of July shall be observed as a general holiday in Warrington. As almost all the schools have already made their ar- rangements for the 30th of June, the suggested ' postponement—if any attempt be made to give it practical effect—is likely to meet with considerable opposition. It is understood that several of the Nonconformist schools have decided not to consent to any alteration in the date, and one school has already had a meeting of teachers aod passed a resolution, to be forwarded to the Mayor, zondemn- ing any suggested postponement. We have received the following letter on the subject:—

Sir,—lf I appear to be somewhat premature in my writing to you, I trust you will excuse me and put it down to my over anxiety on the subject in question. There is a rumour very prevalent in town to the effect that an attempt is going to be made to alter the date of Walking Day from the already appointed time, to Thurs- day, July 6th, the date of the Royal Wedding. As a superintendent of a Sunday school, I wish to make a strong protest against any such change as I believe it would not be received with any degree of favour by the parties for whom Walking Day is instituted. Already arrangements are made by most schools, certainly by ours, and if a date were chosen other than the one we have arranged for, our loss would be such that we should have to abandon our usual programme. Many schools and institutions, have made such arrangements with the railway companies that cannot be altered, and they roust carry them out or lose the guarantees they have made.

If any attempt i 3 nrsie to form a " big "procession then with more earnestness I protest. I never had a harder day of pleasure than Walking Day, 1887, and as a scholars' treat it was a farce. I venture to say there are many Sunday School teachers in Warrington, who if nothing else serves to remind them of our noble Queen's Jubilee year, will remember it by the miserable two hours spent in the park waiting for the procession to be put into shape, or by the miserable parade of tired children passing round the town and then the remainder of the day spent in any fashion but an enjoyable one, owing to everyone being bad tempered because of the morning's unsatisfactory proceedings. It may be very nice for the town's officials to figure prominently, but it is not their day, it is the children's day, and why should it be humbugged to such a degree ? Doubtless many reasons sould be adduced not to change, but these are mine. I sincerely hspe I may be premature, and that no step will be taken to alter this festival.— Yours truly, S. F.

I meeting of the Lymm Local Board was held on Tues- day evening,"when there were present—Mr. James Greg- son (chairman), and Messrs. H. Harrison, J. Holt, R. Whitelegg, E. Collies, thanking the Board for the use of the Bridgewater room for his Bible class during the past winter and asking for a continuance of the use of the room for the class. The application was granted.—lt, was stated that there was only one case now at the Newfield Hospital, and the patient, who was convales- cent, would probably be able to leave the hospital at the end ot the week.—Mr. James Holt proposed, and it was seconded and carried, that when the present patient was discharged, the nurse be dismissed, and the hospital thoroughly cleaned nut.—Mr. Rouse, captain of the fire brigade, reported that certain persons who rang the fire bell on the occasion of the recent fires in the village had applied to him for remuneration, but he had been unable to pay them as no resolution had been passed by the Board authorizing him to do so.—Mr. Harrison said that he was of opinion that such a resolution had been passed by the Board at one time.—Mr. Ridgway stated that he could not remember any such resolution, but it was tug rule in other places to renutnerate those who rang the fire bell on the outbreak of a fire. The matter was referred to a committee.—A letter was read from the County Council granting £267 Os. 10d. for the main- tenance and £45 83. 7d. for the improvement of the main roads.—ln his report the Gas Manager, Mr. Severs, stated that the protuction of gas during the past month had been 675, bOO cubic feet, against 612,000 cubic feet for the corresponding month of last year, which was an increase of 63,000 cubic feet. This was all the business of public interest.

CRICICRT.

It will not do for Mr. R. Smith to absent himself often from the ranks of the Warrington first eleven, else they will soon have a record of " lost games." He was not with them in the match against Wigan on Saturday, and our home eleven were beaten by 96 runs to 69. Per- haps some logician will say it does not matter, as both teams got a total with a 9 and a 6, only Warrington put their 6 before the 9. However,there is little doubt that had Smith been present and made anything like an average performance, the tables would have been turned. I allude in this somewhat eulogistic manner to Mr. Smith's value to the club chiefly 'oecause the club,taking it right thiough, is deficient in good batters. Clarke for instance made the highest score of 21,and that score is unusual for the general rank and file of the club. In bowling and fielding the eleven is certainly adept—but this will not serve ; there must be some one who can "pile the figures on."

The bowling was all done by Sugg and J. J. Crosfield. They each took five wickets, the latter for 27 runs and the former for 39 runs. S. M. Crosfield was rather " off colour," BO to say, and in his five oven was unable to take a wicket. Well, even the best of bowlers stumble across periods when the deftness of his throw takes a temporary respite. By the way, I wonder did Mr. Sing see a portrait—a black smudged square—labelled Sugg, the professional," which appeared in a contem- porary on Saturday. If he did see it, did he recognise himself I wonder?

At home here, despite the plentiful presence of " Old Man Pluvius "grog, which fell " in soft, gentle drops of sweet refreshing rain," the 2nd eleven managed to get the crow of Wigan 2nd. But it was not a tight battle. Warrington came out with a total of 88 for seven wickets while their opponents landed 85 for the full team. Jefferson and Bibby were again the "monster " scorers for Warrington ; indeed they were also the bowlers, and hence are making themselves two indis- pensable players. Conjointly they scored 50 runs.

The Sankey Wesleyans experienced their first reverse of the season on Saturday, Brick-street and Friars Green doing the trick. This team with the formidable name, is no doubt a good team, but it was indifferent fielding that lost the Wesleyans the game. This is well borne out by the number of extras, no less than 19 being scored against them. From " hits " the Wesleyans are actually in a majority of one. Low scoring was the order of the day the highest score in both teams being nine, from W. Barton's bat. Sudlow and Burton, eight each, closely followed by Bailey seven were the pick of the Wesleyans. Critchley was responsible for six of the Wesleyan wickets, and Watkins (4), Bailey (3), helped in the dismissal of the Brick-street, etc.

While the first Wesleyans were hastening to their downfall, the second string were playing " ducks and drakes " with Brick-street second. Just look at the record! Wesleyans 2nd, 76 for five wickets; Brick-street all out 16. J. Booth ran up a scare of 60 and was not out along with F. Light 12, when the innings were declared closed. The Brick-street team had a complete collapse, and the fowl known as ducks were very com- mon. Seven of tho batsmen actually managed to make no runs between them ! Az. might be expected the Wesleyan bowlers had an excellent analysis. Booth was not satisfied with scoring "half a century " not out, but actually took seven wickets for seven runs, J. Roderick also taking two wickets for six runs. The second team believe in making surprises, as in the last match they played, there was a complete contrast to the present one.

I offer my compliments to the Bank-street Mission ! They are going to set about business now, I can see. I was told last week that one of my observations made recently had touched them in tender places for "it was not exawrkly what oi said as the nawsty why oi said it." So one of them said; and they there and then vowed to achieve fame and honourasile distinction. Very glad to hear it. And they started on Saturday by un- mercifully beating Widnes's Red Nose—l mean Rose—by 23 runs for three wickets to nine for 11 wickets. That was good.

Dear me ! What next ! Here's the Trinity Cricket : Club been shamefully abusing that collective " apple of my eye," the Workingmen's Mission team. The delicate little team of cricketing genius of which 1 so often speak in rapturous terms. 1 don't know what they did to them exactly, maybe they refused to supply refresh- ments. At any rate the Mission team scored 22 for 11 wickets and when the other team had playfully rim up 16 for three wickets, the Mission players are described as having left under protest. How sad !

MOOR]; WALKING DAT. The Wesleyans of Voore held their annual Walk- ing Day on Thursday. The scholars, about 130 in number. met at the Wesleyan Chapel, at two o'c'ock, and marched in procession through the vil- lage, headed by the Warrington Blue Coat School Band, and accompanied by the Superintendents, Roylance, Leech, and Banat, and t!acher- of the Sunday School. They called and sang at the following houses :—Miss Rowlands,Mr. sloane,Miss Dutton,Mis. Jones of the" Elms," and Mrs. Massey, Acton Grange,after which they adjlurned to a field kindly lent for the occasion by Mr. Roylance. Games and races for prizes were indulged in until about halt-past eight p.m., wnen the party broke up.

BT LUKE BLUNT.

There are two institutions to which turn the thoughts of English people, urgently in want of cash. Those institutions are: the pawnbroker's shop and the loan office. The former is the oldest institution and receives the most patronage. Since the daya of Adam, I have been told, borrowing has been one of the ever increasing accomplishments of mankind. But it was left to the Jews of Lom- bardy, in the early centuries, to formulate a proles- sion of money lending—or pawnbroking. Borrow- ing had grown considerably with the arrival of the Normans in this country; then, every body borrowed from everybody else, till at last there was no one that had a groat to lend, and there was a danger of borrowing becoming an obsolete art until the born- I bardian Jews arrived with their money bags, in ILondon, and prevented this imminent chance of a sad and depressing national calamity. The nobles used to patronize them most; but the money was always borrowed upon a promissory note. This state of thing t did not exist long; the Lombards received too much encouragement to be content to lie around like sleepy clams. It was nothing for a young spark of a noble to borrow a few hundreds and give as many promissory notes; and it was commoner still for him to add so:iiability to the bargain. Ile wuuld sometimes take a few of his companions and hunt about a trite for a grey- bearded LombarA who had lent him money; and when they found him they would fun round with him considerably, and tickle his venerable old sides in a playful way, with a little dirk. The results were the same, every time; no claim was made by She Lombard any more, for a return of the loan. Owing to some hundreds of these nobles once deny- ing their promissory notes--evidently out of fun— the Lombards got suspicious and wouldn't lend a groat without security of a substantial kind such as jewellory, and things. At the same time they all assumed the sign of the three balls over their shops.

It was this sign that I first began to investigate when I opened an inquiry into the workings of local pawnshops, the other week. I asked many learned persons, including some managers, what the sign signified. The answers were diverse and some of them irrelevant, but all amounted to this: " I don't know." Fired si ith an ambition to solve the problem I went to the Library and plunged into the pages of " Notes and Queries." I found several explanations, all in keeping with the complacent, simple, asinine lying of the period, from which are handed down a few score of our interesting little fables. And they all had touching stories of ideal heroism. One stales—in volume nine I think—that the three bulls are emblematic of Faith, Hope, and Charity, and taken from the beautiful story of how Christ, passing the house of a poor destitute man, threw through tli?. window tbree gold pieces. Then another definition, written in the solemn,, sed ucti ve language of three centuries ago, stated in a most plausible way that once Sr. Nicholas knew in the city of Pantlwra, a poor, blind, decrepit old man, who had three beautiful daughters. starvation was running them close, and resource to a degraded life was the only loophole the girls had from a cruel death from want. So the venerable saint hearing of this was stirred to pity and decided to help them. But see how lie does it ! Instead of going in a staid straightforward way as any man, especially a saint, should on such an errand, the fable describes how St. Nicholas stole up to the house one moonlight night, and having climbed up the flower boxes on to the window sill, flu'ng three bags of gold amongst the sleeping beauties. It was ! to commemorate this that the Lombard pawnbroker !selected the merciful sign, so says the second defin- ition. There are several more ; all equally pretty, and inconsistent. I left, with the small tenacious hope I had long fostered, of proving the veracity of my forefathers, mutilated, and tangled, and broken.

Early on the following Monday morning I re- paired to a well known pawn shop in town. Now, there is a considerable amount of humbug and ignorance circulating in the minds of the general public respecting the transactions of pawnbrokers and the character of tkeir clients. In the ordinary and respectable ratepayer there has generally ap- peared to me to be a great repulsion to the pawn- shop—a repulsion in its way which is equivalent to that which is more justly entertained with regard to the Workhouse. To that highly respectable fled- fearing ratepayer there presents itself in connec- tion with any transaction with the popular " uncle," an odium and disgrace, shameful and un- pardonable. He can only conceive of one class of people having dealings at the "pledge hous.c ;" and they are the people who, having reduced their sub- stance by evil habits, at length put the coping stone upon their social debasement and pauperisation, by realizing upon their personal property, to have a last " fling ' in at the evil indulgence which has ; spelled their deqruction. He also squanders a con- ; siderable amount of compassion on the " poor lost sheep."

There is a fixed community of something like 2,000 persons in Warrington who weekly accept loans under the Lombard's sign. This, of course, is taking into account the business done at each pawn shop. It is not a system of promiscuous pawning that goes on ; the people are not all pledging with the intention of raising a drink. 011, dear no! They are working along in a system of household financial policy which certainly is not at all bril- liant or thrifty. It is about as good as the late Government's foreign policy. Most of those two thousand persons 1 have named, are to be met regularly on a Monday morning taking the pledge'— not the pledge—but the article for pa Irn, and regu- larly they can be met on the Saturday bringing the said article home again. And week after week you will see the same person with the same article ; a woman will take her husband's trousers from the week they are new, till they have been worn out by wearing and conveyance to the pawn shop. Then new pair is purchased, and the same thing goes on again—goes on again maybe until her husband dies; then all the recalling goes in of a heap, probably with the true and genuine remark to the manager " Begorra! lle'e did! He is gawn—and there's no- body will kape these dark reminders of his happy toile better than yourself." So goods of that charac- ter are otter, left for years—till the "old gun's been elected," as the son puts it, when he comes to pre- pare for her funeral. But the pledging is really more an inveterate habit with the majority of those 2,000 Warrington people, than a necessity. It is almost solely done by the women, and horn ex- , perienced managers I can gather that th6re are two reasons chiefly for the regular weekly appearance of th• a p 3ople. The first reason is that the habit ,1 has been handed down as a family legacy. Since ! they culd rememberenany 3f tire women are ex are that all their reepectehle relatives, including their parents, never missed taktne lather's pants to the " storehouse " on a Monday—and before he in - gutted about them on a Sunday, haying them out ! again. Why t heir relatives did it they do not know —they are simply content, to pin their faith to the custom. Others start the habit upon their own responsibility. Perhaps a labouring man in the re- ceipt of a El a week can! just sctape through the expenses for a week's keep with that sum. One day he will have:a misfortune—probably bury a child, or lose two or three days' work, or, maybe , buy a rabbit dog cheap ; do something any way, which will involve an expenditure of some shill- ings beyond hie income. So he says to his wife, that she might put things straight with his Sunday • clothes—or her own as the case maybe, for per- Imps there flint more than one of them with best ' clothes. Well, new.evnat is the result? It is an impossibility for them to reclaim those clothes for ever more and be straight financially out of next week's SI. They know this, and can only see one thing for it----get them out on a Saturday and put them in nemin on the Monday. The idea of saving a few pence each week does not seem to strike them. So they get out of the debt in a way which becomes perfectly clear and clever to them. They start pro- bably on a new jacket Fuld get Si. lent ; it decreases in value, and only 6e. is lent, and gradually it de- creases to a is. loan, and decreases till it isn't good any more and no loan can he obtained. So you see ! the 84.balanceon the wrong side has gradually been diminished by such a simple thing as the deteriora- tion of the value of the article. This eatifies them and they keep it U.

The rate of interest charged is it halfpenny tier month on every two shilling, or part of two shill- In addition to this a ticket ia made ouZ: for each p!e.lge. and this costs a halfpenny. If the left on the hands of the pawnbroker for p2ci ntis without being redeemed, he can sell it by auction. Now here cornea an important point in the pawnbroking huAne.ss. When the pledges are being taken in on a Monday, the manager who ' values the bundles has no time to enter into any calculations about the value of the :article before

him. Experience has to serve him. One moment's examination has to suffice and he says what he will lend on the article. Of course the pledger has already asked for a price—probably one which covers the full value of tha article, lithe manager makes a slip and gives that price he is generally going to lose 50 per cent, upon that deal. It is this way. There is scarcely.a regular pledger who does not look with religious fervour upon any pro- ject for doing a tharp trick upon the manager. Consequently.thev are always testing him. If he take tile article upon which is asked a loan cover- ing its total value, he is "spooled," The article will never be redeemed ; he will lose the accumu- lated twelve month's interest. 3 here is always a sale counter at a pawnshop where these unredeemed articles are offered for sale ; but in a case like the one I have mentioned ",he price originally asked will be too much to procure a purchaser. So the article drifts away amongst the surplus stock, and in time comes under the auctioneer's hammer. Per- haps four shillings was lent on it; when the auctioneer Las finished with it the pawnbroker gets about one shilling. So he has to be caretul and as a place with a good business in Warrington. takes in as natty as a thousand bundles on a Mon- day,thiare are not many hours allowed the manager for valuing purposes.

--- Some people think that only poor people deal with the pawnbroker, even in Warrington. This is not so. Many of the middle class, and even the better class have, so I find, a venerable regard for "my uncle." But they do not go to his shop with bundles. Oh, dear no ! This is how it is done. Suppose I over drew my banking account—i.e.. if I had one—l should drop a note down to the manager of a pawnbroking establishment and ask for some- one to come up to receive a pledge. The manager would arrive in due time all prim and respectable, with a black bag containing money and pledge books. This transaction is simply a business one; I merely want to get straight with my banking account. I always tell him This several times. He will value the pictures or jewellery I offer and if the loan is over £lO we make special terms of inter- est. Then he enters the loan ; but where? Not in the ordinary boo:: required for ordinary pledges by Act Parliament; not in the book where it can be seen by the assistants ; not in any book that goes into the hands of anyone but that trustworthy manager. You get the money ; the next day a respectable youth comes and takes the articlos "into custody "; you send for the manager again when you want to redeem and not even your next door neighbour gets an idea of the transaction. So you can go on in comfort- shuddering always when the pawnshop is men- tioned—no one will find you out. But there are many strange happenings in these loan shops and so I propose next week to give an account of a day's sojourn in a local pawnshop.

BOWLS.

The bowling green at the Liberal Club has been put into capital condition and for some time borne matches have been going on. For next Saturday but one how- ever, a match has been arranged with the Winnington Liberal Club bowling team.

In the Warrington and District League I notice the Bulls Head Club are keeping pretty well to the front. They are the only team who have won two matches. They have only played two; the Red Lion haite played three and won one, the Cemetery and Bank Quay have played two each and won none, and the Horse and Jockey have played one and won one. The Red Lion won their one match on Saturday by beating Bank Quay.

CYCLING.

It has been the event of the ; it has broken the depressing monotony of a week's work, which cyclists always complain of. It is not every day that G. I'. Mills travels through Warrington to smash a "Lands End to John o' Groats" record. So when he did come through on Tuesday there was scarcely a wlieelman in Warrington who did not turn out to view him. And it was good to examine him. Ile is a finely physiqued young man of 24 and stands abeut six feet high with limbs and thews which bespeak the thorough athlete. He landed at the N.,rton Arms about 1.45. His father was there—so was his sister. The hundreds of people cheered him and admired him and he was just for all the world like the hero in a martial romance.

Once he did the same run on an ordinary bicycle in five days, one hour, and 45 minutes, and than he went the same distance on a tricycle in five days, ten hours and no minutes. It was this record that he has been going in to break during this week. The total distance is 876 miles. He set out from Lands End on Monday morning at 8 a.m., and he was mounted upon a splen- did pneumatic tyred tricycle weighing about 35Ihs. He had travelled 357 miles when he arrived in Warring- ton—in very little more than the time it takes to get by rail down to Lands .End.

He was accompanied to Warrington by Lawrence Fletcher, B. 11. Cobble, and T. Edge, all of the Anfield Club, who paced Mills. They also accompanied him northwards and he reached Granton Ferry, 17 miles north of Edinburgh at 1 p.m., on Wednesday. This was a distance of 582 miles frim Land's End. He was then six hours ahead of the safety record and 29 hours ahead of his former tricycle record. Ho had only 294 miles to do then.

Th plucky rider finished his journey at 10.47 yester- day morning, making his time 3 days, 16 hours, 47 min- ut. a. This is 7 hours 8 minutes ahead of the safety bicycle record, and 1 day 17 hours 13 minutes ahead of ti-s) solid tyred tricycle.

Knutsford was eventu- ally reached and then the return was made.

The members of the Wneelers Club had worse luck. They set out for Sale and were overtaken by the storm at Lymm. They (lid not stop at a smithy, but at another place. ltut although the weather cleared up they did not reach Sale; they did not attempt.

By the way, I have only just found out that there are lady members in the Wheelers Club. Now surely there ip going to be a chance of a ladies' club being formed. GOLF.

ANNUAL MEETING OF THE WARRING- TON PRIMROSE LEAGUE.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Habita- tion of the Primrose League, was held in the Con- servative Club, Sankey-s., on Wednesday night. Mrs. Reynolds, the Lady Ruling Councillor of the Habitation, took the chair, and she was supported on the platform by Mr. R. Fierpoint. M.P., Alder- men Harrison, Sutton, and Francomb, Councillors Muir, Smethurst, Hutchinson, and Hill, Captain J. 1). Buckton, Dr. Spinks, and Messrs. It. Davies J. Roberts and W. Diggle. On the proposition of Alderman Harrison, seconded by Councillor Smet- hurst, Mrs. Rsynolds was re-elected Lady Ruling Councillor tor the forthcoming year, and she was also thanked for her serviees to the League during the past year. Alderman Harrison moved that Mr.C.H.Wood be hon. Sec. ,and Messrs.Lowis W.Voisey,J.Roberts, J.Cook- son, j. flampson, G. Pilling, R. H. Dainteth, Alderman Harrison, Mrs. Grounds, Mrs.R.Johnson, Miss Reynolds, Miss S. Reynolds, and Miss Voisey be the Cauncil of the Habitation for the coining year. Mr. Cookson seconded and they were elected unanimously.—Alderman Fran- comb then moved the following resolution "That in the opinion of this meeting, the bill nola More Parliament, for the granting of a separate legislature to Ireland, is not in accordance with the will of the British people, and if passed would be an injustice to us, and would probably cause civil war in Ireland." Mr. Diggle seconded, and the resolution was passed unanimousiy.— The following were elected wardens, on the motion of Alderman Francomb, seconded by Mr. Key : Town Hall Ward, Mrs. &not huist ; WhitQcross %Yard, Mrs. R. Johnson ; Be wsey Ward, Miss Atherton, Orford Ward, Miss Edgar ' • Howley Ward, Mrs. Reynolds ; St. Austin's Ward, Miss Veisey, and Miss Fairhurst. Mr. Robert Davies at this stage took the chair, and he immediately called upon Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P., to address the meet - Mg:

Mr. Pierpoint, who was received with much en- thusiasm, said that he would give them that night an account of how a day in Parliament was spent. They began at three o'clock on Monday's, Tuesday's and Thursday's. If a member wished to have a seat he must be there in good time, at the beginning of prayers. Then there were various small matters of private busi- ness, such as presentations of petitions. On Tuesdav,he had the honour of presenting a petition for the Warring- ton district agaimt the Welsh Suspensory Bill. At about 3.30 began questions, and there were far more of these than appeared in the papers. They averaged between 60 and 70 daily. Then began the Borne Rule Bill, which dragged slowly along. Divisions came over and over again, and when one considered that every division took a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes, a considerable amount of time was taken up by this means. At 12 o'clock at midnight the debate was adjourned. The House did not adjourn even then. A climber of gentle- men were anxious to get their little bills tur, ugh during the unopposed time. Some of these bills were bad ones, and all that was necessary to stop them was to say " I object." The House generally adjourned at 12.30, and the member got to bed some where about 2 o'clock. He had alluded to the Home Rule Bill dragging wearily along, and this was well shown, as they had only got half way through the third clause, and there were 40 in all. If the bill gets through this year, the Goverment will be very happy. Mr. Gladstone was followed by his party with great unwillingness, and he believed that they were expecting the House of Lords to throw it out. They were leaving it for other people to do what they dare not do themselves. The legislation of the Government was almost all class legislation,as witness the Employers' Liability Bill, the Budget, the Vaccination Bill, the Local Veto Bill, the Home Rule Bill and the Welsh Sus- pensory Bal. He also contended that the Local Veto Bill and the Welsh Suspensory Bill were confiscatory bills.

After Mr. Pierpoint's speech, 'a concert was held at which the following programme was submitted : Piano- forte dug, " Italy," by Misses Voisuy and Knowles; dance, by the Misses Reynolds, encored ; song," Angus McDonald," by Miss Fanshaw, who was encored ; piano- forte and oboe 'duct, "Selections from the Maritana," Messrs. G. Riley and Ward ; seng, " Death of Nelson," Mr. W. Howard ; pianoforte solo, Miss Voisey. The entertainment closed with a ventriloquial en' ertainment by Mr. A. Grounds. Mr. G. Riley was the accompanist.

AT OUR LOCAL CaURCHES, WARRINGTON.

MOORE WESLEYAN CHAPEL. The anniversary services of the Moore Wesleyan Chapel were held on Sunday last, when the preacher in the afternoon and evening was the Rev. T. Brookes, of Sheffield, formerly stationed in the Warrington Circuit. There were good congregations at each service,especially at the evening service. Special hymns and anthems were given by the choir and children, and the collections for the day amounted to £l6 4s. 6d., being an increase of £2 Ms. on the previous year. THE P.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE. There was again a large attendance at the Pleasant Sunday afternoon mEeting in Wycliffe Church on Sun- day afternoon. The membership of the brotherhood has now reached 610. Mr. S. B. Stringer was the chair- man for the afternoon,and delivered an earnest address. Mr. Power, a workingman from Manchester, and super- intendent of a Ragged Sunday School there, also ad- dressed the meeting, and the address was much appre- ciated. Mrs. R. H. Pearson was the soloist, and ex- quisitely rendered two solos " Rock of ages "and " The Better Land," and merited the applause she received. The latter solo was sung to the double accompaniment of 'cello and organ, and produced a fine sffect. Mr. H. Bond played the 'cello, and W. H. Payton presided at the organ. The Rev. John Yonge and Mr. J. Kenworthy also took part in the meeting.

THE FEAST OF CORPUS CHRISTI. The annual feast of Corpus Christi was celebrated in the various Roman Catholic places of worship in War- rington. On Sunday, St. Mary's Buttermarket-street, was crowded in the evening, when the usual procession took place, the Blessed Sacrament being carried round the church under a gorgeous canopy, borne by four soldiers. There was a large number of children and young women dressed in white with white veils, the former carrying bunches of choice flowers. Before the Blessed Sacrament walked a number of children who carried baskets of flowers, which they strewed along the aisles. Father Turner was the officiating clergyman, and was assisted by Father Wray and Duggan. A similar procession took place at St. Alban's Church. During the procession, the children and choir sang Mozart's "Ave Verum " in an excellent manner 'f h e Benediction music was composed by Father Murphy, formerly of Warrington. The choir was under the con- ductorship of Mr. Richard Eccles, and Mr. Mather pre- sided at the organ, performing the difficult selections in a manner which reflected considerable credit upon him. BUCKLEY-STREET ME PHODIST FREE CHURCH. The anniversary services in connection with the above church were held on Sunday. The preacher in the morning and evening was Mr. Walter L. McNair of Liverpool (secretary of the Liverpool Railway Mission). The preacher took as his text in the morning Psalm 116, verses 12 and 13. He said that lie found it rather diffi- cult to preach a set sermon in the chapel, as he had been accustomed to preaching out in the open air, but he felt it an honour to be invited to preach the anniversary sermons in Buckley-st. Chapel. It was fitting on that occasion to ask, like the Psalmist : " What shall I ren- der unto the Lord for His benefits towards me ?" In these times the question generally arose in the minds of men, and very often it was more difficult to remember the mercies of G ai than the chastisements of God. This was a misfortune of our nature, flow often did the tongue speak out a long list of trying times in our ex- perience, but when asked to sing, or speak or pray, the tongue becaine dumb, and they were seldom able to tell of God's goodness, when they ought to pour our their heart in thankfulness and praise for His wonderful deal- ings. Itwas this spirit that made the Psalms so refresh- ing to read. They should not be afraid to ask God for He delighted in giving, and they could naver ask too much. A spirit of thankfulness was what God required in return. He appealed to the congregation to show their sense of gratitude by giving freely towards the funds of the chapel. Mr. McNair preached again in the evening to a good congregation. Special hymns were arranged for the services, and tin-i choir under the able leadership of Mr. Andrew Jennings gave an anthem "Talk with us Lord "in the morning in a very credit- able manner, and again in the evening, they gave " Daughter of Zion "in an equally clever style. Mr. John J. Fairhurst, was the organist.

• -4,--. THE WARRINGTON MAGISTRATES AND SCHOOL PROSECUTIONS.

Richard Gou!den, Heath-road. Penketh, was brought before Alderman Harrison (chairman), Councillor 11. Roberta, and, Mr. %V. yierpoint, at the Warrington Borough C kurt, yesterday, summoned for not sending his- daughter regularly to school. Mr. B. Shearer, who prosecuted, said that this else had been adjourned in May for a month, as the defendant had produced a doctor's certificate, which certified that his daughter was suffering from pneumonia. She had been a very bad at tender of school for a number of years. —Councillor H. Roberts said that there had been some reference made by Mr. Shearer, and by the Clerk to the School Attendance Committee of Sank ey,the tenour of the remarks being to the effect that the magistrates did not support the school attendance officera in their eases be- fore the court. As he (Councillor Roberts) was one of the magistrates on the occasion in which the present case was heard, all he would say was, that neither for" the school attendance officers, nor the Board of Gear- aians, nor the Rural Sanitary Authority, nor anybody else, would he be a party in the face of evidence which was not controverted, that the child was seriously ill at the time, in administering the law in a way calculated to bring those who administlred it into contempt. Here was a child who was suffering from an ailment, and she was thirteen years of age. It was perfectly well known how serious the nature of the complaint was. For the school attendance officer to find fault with them, in the course they took, in acting upon the dietates of butnanity,rather than the stern requirements of the law, was in his opinion unjustifiable. It was calculated to prejudice in the minds of the public, the action of the magistrates who acted upon this particular case. Ile knew nothing about the general conduct of the magis- trates. He was defending himself from which appeared to him to be an unfair action.—Mr. Shearer said the child was summoned for a time previousto the date of. the certificate, and there was no evidence produced to show that the child could not go to school for six months previously. The parents were summoned on the Ist May and there was no complaint of ill-health then. Seven days afterwards they got a certificate. The child had ma.ie very poor attendances for five years.—Coun- cillor Roberts said that a complaint like pneumonia did not come in a few months. The father had stated that the girl had been a weakling from birth. She was not suffering from an ordinary illness.—Mr. Shearer con- tended that the father was not on oath, but the magis- trates' clerk explained that he could not take oath, as he was the defendant.—Mr. Shearer finally withdrew the case.

ST. B.A.IINABAS' SCHOOLS.

SETTLEMENT OF THE DISPUTE. The dispute between the Rev. G. Allton, vicar of St. Barnabas, and the managers of the day schools, which has been going on during the past few years. and has been the cause of several lawsuits, has at length been settled. The vicar has resigned his position a.; chairman of the School Committee, and agreed to recognise Messrs. R. Proud and T. Pil king- ton as duly elected managers of the school. lie will, we understand, in tha future take no part whatever in the management of the day schools. Mr. R. Proud will be the treasurer and correspond- ent. The agreement between the two parties was drawn up, and signed on Thursday afternoon.
